Script → shot list (parallel with #2)** Kimi K2.6 generates the script from a concept. But LTX takes shot-level prompts, not dialogue — so you need a second pass that decomposes "scene at the diner where George complains about X" into a list of shot prompts (camera, who's in frame, action, ~5s each). I'd structure this as two LLM calls: concept → script, script → shot JSON. Worth prototyping early with the baseline LTX so you know what prompt shape actually works. **4. Timeline assembly** Once #1–#3 land, this is mostly plumbing: run the shot list through LTX+LoRA, drop the resulting clips into the timeline composition in order, optionally with the dialogue as overlaid audio (TTS?) or text. Your timeline tool already exists — this is the cheapest piece. **Suggested sequencing:** - Week 1: #1 baseline + start #2 data prep + prototype #3 against baseline - Week 2: #2 training runs + refine #3 with real LTX feedback - Week 3: #4 assembly + iterate on weakest link **Biggest risk to call now:** character consistency across cuts. If a "scene" is 6 shots and Jerry looks like a different person in each one, the whole thing falls apart regardless of how good the script is. Worth deciding upfront: are you okay with style-only Seinfeld vibes (looks like the show, characters are generic), or do you need recognizable Jerry/George? Good — and the consistency-via-prompt-vocabulary framing actually makes this much more tractable than character-identity LoRAs. The key insight is that your *caption schema* and your *inference prompts* need to share the same vocabulary. That's the lever. Let me think through the meta-tool with that as the pivot. **The pipeline as discrete stages** (orchestrator task list): 1. **Criteria spec** — the input config. A structured doc with: bucket targets (e.g., "200 clips Jerry's apartment, 150 Monk's diner, 100 street, 50 Kramer's apartment"), per-bucket inclusion rules (what the VLM is checking — characters present, shot type, lighting), and a caption template that names the scene/costume/action in the *exact* vocabulary you'll prompt with at inference. Get this right first; everything else serves it. 2. **Search** — agent issues YouTube queries scoped to the criteria ("Seinfeld apartment scene full", episode lists, fan compilations). Outputs a candidate URL list. 3. **Download** — yt-dlp video+audio (extend your existing audio util). Episode-level, not clip-level yet. 4. **Cheap pre-filter** — before any VLM cost: PySceneDetect splits episodes into shots, drop anything <2s or >10s, optionally audio-transcript-keyword filter ("Jerry", location cues). Cuts the VLM bill 5–10x. 5. **VLM judge + bucket** — for each surviving clip, VLM answers: "Does this match bucket X criteria? If yes, which bucket?" Output: clip → bucket or reject. 6. **VLM caption** — only on kept clips, using the caption template from step 1. Same vocabulary you'll prompt with. 7. **Bucket fill loop** — if any bucket is short, agent goes back to step 2 with refined queries. This is where the orchestrator earns its keep. 8. **Manifest export** — clip paths + captions, ready for LTX 2.3 training. **Where it lives:** You're right that the split is: executable primitives in vibecomfy (download node, scene-segment node, VLM-judge node, VLM-caption node — each a workflow), orchestrator + criteria spec + task list in Astrid. Astrid drives, vibecomfy executes. Bonus: the same primitives compose into any future dataset, not just Seinfeld. **Three things worth flagging:** - **Caption schema is the load-bearing piece.** If captions say "Jerry sitting on couch in apartment" but you later prompt "Jerry, white sneakers, blue shirt, in Jerry's apartment, medium shot," the model won't have learned the mapping. I'd write five example inference prompts you want to work, then reverse-engineer the caption template from those. Don't skip this. - **Pre-filter before VLM, always.** Naïvely VLM-judging every shot in 50 episodes is thousands […]

> AGENT

OK now I can answer concretely — and the answer's actually better than I thought. **Executor = the capability.** It's "fetch Reigh data," "render a timeline," "judge a clip with a VLM." One unit of work, independently runnable, takes artifacts in and produces artifacts out. Agnostic to *who* runs it. **When you wire executors into a task plan via an orchestrator**, each step is one of exactly three kinds (this is the V1 task-mode design in `docs/orchestrator-v1-plan.md`): | Step kind | Who does it | How it advances | |---|---|---| | `code` | nobody — deterministic argv subprocess | runs, gate verifies `produces`, moves on | | `attested` | **an agent or a human** | actor runs the work, acks with identity + evidence, gate verifies `produces` | | `nested` | another orchestrator | delegates to a child plan | So yes — **human tasks are a first-class primitive.** An `attested` step with `--actor <name>` (matching `ARTAGENTS_ACTOR`) is a human step. The human does the work, then runs the ack command, and the gate checks the `produces` artifacts (file exists, schema valid, image has right dimensions, etc.) before the run advances. Self-acks and unpinned actors are rejected — so the evidence trail is real, not a checkbox. `attested` with `--agent <id>` is the same thing but an agent attests instead. Same evidence requirement. Loops/fan-out are `repeat.until` / `repeat.for_each` wrappers on any step — that's how your bucket-fill loop expresses itself, and how "for each candidate video, segment + judge" expresses itself. **Mapping the Seinfeld pipeline to step kinds:** - Search YouTube → `attested` (agent), or `code` if it's a deterministic API call - Download video → `code` - Scene-segment → `code` - VLM judge / caption → `attested` (agent) — produces a structured JSON, gate verifies it against `json_schema` - **"Eyeball 50 random clip+caption pairs before training"** → `attested` (human), produces a sign-off file with notes - Manifest export → `code` - The whole bucket-fill outer loop → orchestrator using `repeat.until(buckets_full)` The human-review gate isn't a nice-to-have you bolt on — it's a native step type with the same evidence/verification machinery as agent steps. That's the part I'd lean into. **One concrete implication for the criteria spec:** since `produces` checks are how the gate validates each step, you'll want the criteria element to also define the *expected output schemas* per stage (judge output schema, caption output schema, manifest schema). > AGENT

Here's the whole thing in one picture. ## The spine: a shared vocabulary One thing connects every stage: a **locked vocabulary** of scenes, characters, costumes, and shot types. Captions use it. Training learns it. Script-to-shot decomposition emits it. Inference prompts consume it. Get this right once, in a single element, and every downstream stage stops being a separate consistency problem. Concretely: ~3 scenes (Jerry's apt, Monk's diner, one more), ~4 characters with 1–2 outfit variants each, ~4 shot types. Small, formulaic, Seinfeld-shaped. ## Four orchestrators in Astrid ### 1. `dataset_build` — produce the training set Owns the bucket-fill loop. Reads a **criteria element** (bucket targets + per-bucket inclusion rules + caption template + output schemas), runs until full. Steps, in order, wrapped in `repeat.until(buckets_full)`: - `code`: YouTube search → candidate URLs - `code`: yt-dlp video download (extend existing audio util) - `code`: scene-segment (reuse `packs/builtin/scenes`) - `code`: cheap pre-filter (length, transcript keywords) - `attested` (agent): VLM judge → bucket assignment, schema-checked - `attested` (agent): VLM caption using locked vocabulary, schema-checked - **`attested` (human): review ~50 random clip+caption pairs** before training kicks off - `code`: manifest export ### 2. `lora_train` — train the LoRA Takes the manifest, runs LTX 2.3 training. Mostly one or two `code` steps wrapping the training script + an `attested` (human) sign-off on eval samples vs. baseline before promoting the LoRA. ### 3. `script_to_shots` — concept → shot list Two `attested` (agent) steps: - Kimi K2.6: concept → script (dialogue + scene headers) - Kimi K2.6: script → shot JSON, **constrained to the locked vocabulary** (only emits scenes/costumes that exist) The vocabulary constraint is what keeps script generation aligned with what the LoRA can render. Schema-check the shot JSON. ### 4. `scene_render` — shots → final video - `repeat.for_each(shot)`: LTX+LoRA generates the clip via vibecomfy (`external.vibecomfy.run`) - Optional: TTS or on-screen text for dialogue - `code`: drop clips into timeline composition in order - `attested` (human): review before final render - `code`: render final video ## How they compose A top-level `seinfeld_demo` orchestrator chains the four as `nested` steps: ``` dataset_build → lora_train → script_to_shots → scene_render ``` But these aren't strictly serial in practice. `script_to_shots` and `scene_render` can be developed and iterated against the *baseline* LTX (no LoRA) before `lora_train` finishes — they only need the vocabulary, not the LoRA itself. That's the parallelization you want. ## What lives where - **Astrid**: criteria/vocabulary element, all four […]

> AGENT

Real frictions I'm seeing in this run — calling them out now since the build is still going: **1. Captions describe a frame, not motion.** We're sending the mid-frame to `visual_understand` for both judge AND caption. For a video LoRA that's wrong — the caption says "george stands by the door while jerry naps" but the clip might be a sequence where george enters, jerry sits up, conversation starts. The training data won't match the motion. We should use `builtin.video_understand` for captions (it ingests a video window with audio, not a single frame, and already uses a `response_schema` internally per its run.py:257). Then captions can include verbs that match what's actually happening across the clip. **2. Clip cutting is bare ffmpeg in run.py — not an executor.** Right now I'm calling `ffmpeg -ss start -t dur ...` inline. That should be a `builtin.clip_cut` executor: in = source + start + end + target dims/fps/audio policy, out = standardized MP4. Then the orchestrator just calls it like any other step. This also gives us one place to: - normalize FPS to 24 (LTX target) - strip audio (LTX doesn't use it for training) - resize to a target resolution per bucket - detect/crop letterboxing (a real problem with YouTube compilations) - re-key at start (clean keyframes) `builtin.shots` exists for "scenes → shot windows" but its semantics may not match — worth inspecting before duplicating. **3. Duration heuristic is broken.** `yt-dlp --flat-playlist` returns `NA` for some videos → parsed as 0s → our "skip if >30min" check passes for ALL unknown-duration videos. Need a real duration probe before downloading or after. **4. No per-bucket near-duplicate detection.** Three different "Seinfeld Monk's compilation" videos likely contain overlapping scenes. We'll train on the same scene multiple times with slightly different ffmpeg cuts. Need perceptual-hash dedup on accepted clips, or content-hash on a keyframe. **5. No video-quality filter.** We accept any resolution. The clip-01.mp4 is 854×480 vp9. Other candidates might be 360p or have a channel watermark in the corner — LoRA will learn the watermark. Need: minimum resolution, watermark detection (or just a manual reject list of channel ids). **6. Letterboxing/pillarboxing.** Compilations often have black bars. Untouched, the LoRA learns "Seinfeld = black bars." Need an autocrop step (ffmpeg `cropdetect`). **7. Scene segmentation misses within-scene hard cuts.** PySceneDetect at default threshold may treat a 2-cut sequence (wide → medium → wide) as one "scene." Our […]

And would it be hard as part of this, like I'm trying to figure out how this would fit in for us to have like a quick, like a really simple HTML interface, you know, for going through this where we press X and then that saves to a JSON, you know, where you just open the page. > AGENT

Yes, easy — and it's exactly the right shape for the human review gate in `project.md`. Two paths: **1. Static HTML + localStorage + "Download JSON" button.** Zero deps, just open the file. Decisions live in browser storage until you click download. Survives refresh. Doesn't auto-save to disk. **2. Tiny local server (Python `http.server` subclass, ~100 LOC).** Serves the HTML + MP4s + manifest, has one POST endpoint that writes `human_review.json` to disk after every keypress. Open `localhost:8000`, review, decisions persist live. Resumable across sessions. I'd go with **(2)** — same effort, much better UX (no losing work if you close the tab, no Downloads-folder JSON wrangling), and it slots cleanly into the orchestrator later as `seinfeld.human_review` executor (the orchestrator just runs the server and blocks until you press a "done" button or the manifest is fully reviewed). Proposed minimal interface: - One clip at a time, autoplaying loop - Caption shown below the video - Keys: `X` = reject, `Space` or `→` = accept-and-next, `←` = back, `Enter` = save-and-skip-to-next-unreviewed - Progress bar: `12 / 30` - Decisions saved to `runs/seinfeld-dataset/human_review.json`: `{clip_id: {decision: "accept"|"reject", reviewed_at: ts, notes?: str}}` - Optional: number keys 1-4 to tag a reject reason (`watermark`, `wrong_scene`, `wrong_character`, `too_short_motion`) Want me to build that? Two things to confirm before I start: 1. **Just accept/reject, or also let you edit the caption inline?** Caption-editing is genuinely useful (the action-clause is often slightly wrong), but adds form-state complexity. I'd start without it and add if you want it. 2. **Run once now (review v0 dataset), or wire it as a proper `seinfeld.human_review` executor with `executor.yaml` + STAGE.md + the produces-gate?** First gets you reviewing in 5 min; second is ~15 min more to do it the Astrid way.

> AGENT

Right call. Generic primitive in Astrid, project-specific HTML on top. Here's the shape I'd build, keeping it as small as possible: **1. `builtin.human_review` — one new executor in core.** Single Python file. Takes: ``` --html <path> # the page to serve as / (or a dir if there's CSS/JS) --data <path> # served at /data.json (the items to review) --serve <prefix>=<dir> # repeatable static mounts (e.g. /clips=runs/seinfeld-dataset/accepted) --state <path> # POST /save writes here (per-keypress partial saves, resumable) --out <path> # POST /submit writes here, server exits, executor returns 0 --port 0 # auto-pick free port ``` Spins up `http.server` on a free port, auto-opens the browser, blocks until `/submit`. ~120 LOC. Zero third-party deps. The page can be anything — its only contract is `fetch('/data.json')` to load items and `POST /save` or `/submit` to send results. Generic enough to be the same executor used for: this review, the LoRA eval-grid gate, the final-cut review in `scene_render`, future arrangement-approval gates, etc. **2. `astrid/packs/seinfeld/dataset_build/review.html` — project-specific UI.** Single file, inline JS/CSS. Loads `/data.json`, plays one clip at a time from `/clips/...`, hotkeys for accept/reject, POSTs to `/save` on every keypress. ~80 LOC. **Why this factoring:** - The HTTP server / browser-launch / partial-save mechanics are exactly the same for every human gate you'd ever want — that's the primitive - The HTML is the variable part — keep it next to the project that uses it, promote to a shared template only if a second project wants the same shape - Both layers are tiny enough that "really, really simple" stays true **One judgment call to make:** do you want the executor to also accept a `--schema <json-schema>` and validate `/submit` against it (so the produces-gate has teeth — malformed reviews get rejected)? It's ~10 extra lines. I'd say yes, since you already use this pattern with `visual_understand --response-schema`. Confirm the shape and I'll build it + run it against the 30 clips so you can review.

> AGENT

**On flexibility:** Yes — fully bidirectional and shape-agnostic. The executor doesn't know what's in `data.json` or what fields the page POSTs back. The HTML decides both. Same primitive serves: dataset review (clip + caption + accept/reject), LoRA eval-grid (4-up of generated clips + pick winner), arrangement approval (timeline preview + go/no-go + per-clip notes), anything else. **On integration with the Seinfeld process:** The review step becomes the **last step in `dataset_build/run.py`**, after the bucket-fill loop. Concretely: ``` dataset_build/run.py: 1. bucket-fill loop → state.json + accepted/*.mp4 + per-clip captions 2. write provisional_manifest.json 3. subprocess: python3 -m astrid.packs.builtin.human_review.run \ --html astrid/packs/seinfeld/dataset_build/review.html \ --data <out>/provisional_manifest.json \ --serve /clips=<out>/accepted \ --state <out>/human_review.json \ --out <out>/human_review.final.json 4. (blocks until user submits) 5. read human_review.final.json, filter accepted clips 6. write final manifest.json (only human-approved clips) ``` The `review.html` lives **inside the seinfeld pack** (`astrid/packs/seinfeld/dataset_build/review.html`) so the project is self-contained: someone who clones the pack and runs the orchestrator gets collection + reviewer + filtered manifest in one command. No separate setup. **Re-running scenarios** — `dataset_build/run.py` already supports the first two via the existing state file. Adding two more is one branch each: | Scenario | Command | Behavior | |---|---|---| | Fresh run | `seinfeld.dataset_build --target 15 --out <dir>` | Empty dir → bucket-fill → review → manifest | | Resume interrupted | same command, same `--out` | Reads `state.json`, picks up where it stopped | | Re-review only (clips already collected) | `... --review-only` | Skip collection, open reviewer on existing `provisional_manifest.json` | | Expand existing dataset | `... --target 30 --out <dir>` (was 15) | Continues bucket-fill from where it was, then re-runs review on the union | The reviewer itself is **always resumable** — `human_review.json` persists per-keypress, so closing the tab and re-opening picks up where you left off mid-review. **One thing to decide now (cheap to change later):** should the orchestrator **block the whole pipeline on the human gate**, or should bucket-fill and review run as independent commands you chain manually? I'd vote *block by default* but add `--no-review` for headless re-runs or scripted CI use — same way Astrid's other "attested human" steps work. The orchestrator's manifest step then only happens after a real human submit. Ready to build it. Confirm and I'll wire all four pieces (executor + HTML + orchestrator integration + the four re-run modes) and run the review against the v0 30-clip dataset.

> DEVELOPER

Yeah, one more thing to think about is say in this case if we want to iterate on this and get other data, is it easy for us to jump back to earlier phases or is that currently assuming that we don't do that?

> AGENT

Honest answer: **right now the pipeline is forward-resumable only — going backwards is "manually delete the artifact you want regenerated."** The current state file tracks `processed_video_ids` and `buckets[].clips[]`. Re-run reads state, skips processed videos, continues filling under-target buckets. That's it. If you change `vocabulary.yaml` or a schema, nothing notices — the existing judgements/captions stay frozen and the new clips get the new vocabulary, so your dataset becomes inconsistent. **Real scenarios that hurt:** | Want to do | Currently you'd have to | Should be | |---|---|---| | Change vocab → re-caption everything | Delete every `.caption.json` and all but `accepted/` | `--redo caption` | | Tighten the judge prompt → re-judge | Delete every `.judge.json` + clear `processed_video_ids` | `--redo judge` | | Replace one bad clip | Edit state.json by hand | Reviewer marks reject → orchestrator backfills | | Add a new bucket to existing run | Re-search only the new bucket | Already works (existing buckets are at-target) | | Reuse already-downloaded videos with new schema | No clean path | `--redo judge` keeps mp4s + scenes.json, redoes only what changed | **Two ways to fix it, escalating effort:** **Cheap (~30 LOC, do now):** Per-phase freshness checks + a `--redo <phase>` flag. Phases are `download | segment | judge | caption | manifest`. Each phase checks for its own artifacts on disk and skips if present. `--redo judge` wipes all `*.judge.json` and re-runs from judge forward. Cuts both directions: easy to redo selectively, easy to add new phases. **Proper (~80 LOC, do when there's a second project doing this):** Content-addressed artifact caching. Hash the inputs to each phase (`judge.json` keyed by `(video_id, scene_index, schema_hash, prompt_version)`). Change the schema → automatic re-judge of everything, no flag needed. This is how training pipelines like DVC or Snakemake work, and it's the right long-term shape — but YAGNI for now if Seinfeld is the only project. **The feedback edge from review:** the reviewer's reject decisions can drive two things — (a) just filter the final manifest (default), or (b) trigger a "top-up" round that re-runs the bucket-fill loop until the *accepted-after-review* count hits target. That's the closed iteration loop. I'd add (a) now and make (b) a `--top-up` flag. **My recommendation:** build the reviewer first (you can review v0 right now and decide if the data shape is even right), and bundle the cheap `--redo` upgrade in the same change […]

Let me sketch what the polished version actually feels like. **The user experience:** ``` $ astrid datasets new seinfeld-lora --template style-lora ``` Scaffolds a project pack: `vocabulary.yaml` with placeholder enums, `search_seeds.yaml` with query templates per bucket, `schemas/` auto-generated from the vocab. One file is the spine — the rest is derived. ``` $ astrid datasets run seinfeld-lora ``` A live terminal dashboard: ``` seinfeld-lora — collecting · v0.3 jerrys_apt 12/30 ████████████░░░░░ $0.23 accept-rate 34% monks_diner 18/30 ███████████████░░ $0.31 accept-rate 41% hallway 3/20 ███░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░ $0.08 accept-rate 18% ⚠ queue: 14 left processed: 23 downloads: 8.2GB $0.62 / $2.00 budget top rejects: watermark(8) wrong-character(5) too-short-motion(3) ``` Bucket fills → browser opens automatically. Review interface is **black background, one clip autoplaying, caption below, three hotkeys**. No clicks. As they reject, the terminal dashboard updates live — "rejected 4 / queuing top-up" — and a second collection round backfills just those slots, biased away from the rejected source channels. They never leave the loop. When done, terminal: ``` seinfeld-lora @ v1 → manifest.json (90 clips, 68 accepted) → exported: manifest.aitoolkit.json, manifest.kohya.json → trace: every clip → source url, search query, judge confidence, reviewer note → next: astrid lora train --dataset seinfeld-lora ``` Two weeks later they want to add a "kramer_apt" scene. They edit `vocabulary.yaml`. They re-run. ``` vocab: v1 → v2 scenes: +kramer_apt affected: 90 captions, 90 judgments must redo re-judge $0.06, re-caption $0.45 proceed? [Y/n] ``` Confirm — Astrid skips redownload and rescene (unaffected), redoes judge + caption for the existing 90 clips, opens reviewer with the *changed* items pre-flagged. They sign off on 84, kick a top-up for kramer_apt. **The architecture that makes this possible:** | Primitive | What it owns | Used by | |---|---|---| | **Content-addressed artifacts** | Each phase's output is keyed by hash of its inputs. Change vocab → automatic invalidation cascade. | Every iteration the user does | | **`builtin.human_review` server** | HTML in, JSON out. Live `/save` + final `/submit`. Schema-validated. Templatable. | Dataset review, eval-grid pick, arrangement approval, anything human-in-loop | | **Vocabulary → schemas generator** | One yaml → judge schema + caption schema + inference-prompt template. Single source of truth. | Caption agent, judge agent, script-to-shots, scene_render | | **Source adapters** | YouTube, local folders, Bilibili, image dirs — all expose `{search, download}` with the same interface. | Multi-source datasets without per-source orchestrator forks | | **Quality gates layer** | Cheap detectors […]

Base directory for this skill: /Users/user_c042661f/.claude/skills/megaplan-model-selection # Profile selection — three questions, three dials Every piece of work answered with three questions. Each question points at one dial. The dials are **independent** — work through each one ignoring the others — then **weigh the three together** holistically to land on a coherent judgment. | | Question | Dial | What it scales | Flag | |---|---|---|---|---| | 1 | What level of raw capability does this need? | **Intelligence tier** | `$/call` | `--profile` | | 2 | What level of process rigor does this need? | **Planning complexity** | `# of calls` | `--robustness` | | 3 | How deeply does each model need to think? | **Depth** | `tokens/call` | `--depth` (with `--phase-model` as the surgical escape hatch) | The dials aren't a mechanical lookup; they're three lenses you apply to the same task, and the answers should fit each other. A high tier with low robustness is usually a mismatch; so is a low tier with `max` depth. When the three feel like they're pulling in opposite directions, the work probably needs to be split. **The dials measure residual complexity, not nominal scope.** Two factors shape residual complexity, pointing in opposite directions: - **Decisions already made decrease it.** A brief where the major design decisions are resolved — architecture chosen, interfaces specified, edge cases enumerated, trade-offs decided — is less complex than the same scope arriving open-ended. The pre-resolved decisions don't disappear; they're paid for upstream by whoever wrote the brief. - **Unknowns remaining increase it.** Even when many decisions are locked, unresolved external API behavior, unmeasured performance characteristics, ambiguous integration targets, or libraries the team hasn't surveyed yet all add complexity the run has to budget for. (That's what `--with-prep` is built for — see below.) When picking a tier, **discount for decisions made and add for unknowns remaining**. A spec-shaped brief with everything known lands a tier lower than the same nominal scope arriving as a sketch; a tightly-defined brief carrying significant unknowns may need either `--with-prep` or a higher tier. **Tightening the brief beats picking a higher tier** — and is often the cheapest way to bring the rubric down a notch. **Defaults to keep in mind:** `--robustness standard`, `--depth` unset (which means the profile's existing depths win — usually `:low` on premium phases), vendor from your config (`claude` if unset). Reach past those […]

Base directory for this skill: /Users/user_c042661f/.claude/skills/hivemind # hivemind A read-only PostgREST endpoint exposes the Banodoco Discord message feed. This is community knowledge about video/image generation that you can't get from official docs — workflow tips, model comparisons, settings tweaks, gotchas, links to Kijai/Ablejones/community workflows. ## Endpoint ``` https://[REDACTED]/rest/v1/message_feed ``` Header (anon publishable key, safe to commit): ``` [REDACTED] ``` ## Schema Each row: | field | type | notes | |----------------|---------|------------------------------------------------------------| | `message_id` | bigint | discord snowflake | | `content` | text | message body — what you search | | `author_name` | text | display name; `null` for some bot/system messages | | `channel_name` | text | scope your search by channel (see list below) | | `channel_id` | bigint | rarely needed | | `guild_id` | bigint | always Banodoco | | `reactions` | jsonb | usually `null` — don't rely on it for ranking | | `created_at` | timestamptz | ISO 8601 | ## Channels worth knowing Highest signal: - `daily_summaries` — bot-curated daily roll-ups by **BNDC**. **Start here** for any topic — these are pre-distilled summaries with attributions and Discord links. - `wan_chatter`, `wan_comfyui`, `wan_gens`, `wan_resources` — Wan / Wan Animate - `ltx_chatter`, `ltx_resources`, `ltx_gens`, `ltx_training` — LTX 2.x - `comfyui` — generic ComfyUI tips - `resources` — pinned community resources - `vibecoding` — tooling/dev chatter - `training_control_loras` — LoRA training know-how Lower signal: `chatter`, `nsfw` (yes there's tech advice in there but be selective), `hunyuanvideo`, `qwen-image`, `chroma`, `flux`, `z-image`, `magi`, `ace-step`, `kandinsky-5`, `seedance`, `hunyuanvideo`, `top_gens`, `art_sharing`. ## Power users to watch These names come up repeatedly with authoritative answers — weight their messages higher: - **Kijai** — author of the WanVideoWrapper / most ComfyUI nodes for Wan/LTX. Treat as canonical. - **Ablejones** — context windows, color matching, native Comfy integrations, PRs to ComfyUI core. - **djbfilmz** — heavy Wan Animate user, mocap / reskinning experiments. - **42hub** — curates the [wanx-troopers.github.io](https://wanx-troopers.github.io/) knowledge base. - **BNDC** — the daily-summary bot. ## Query patterns (PostgREST) Always URL-encode spaces (`%20`). Use `Accept: application/json` implicitly. ### 1. Basic substring search ```bash curl -s "https://[REDACTED]/rest/v1/message_feed\ ?select=content,author_name,channel_name,created_at\ &content=ilike.*wan%20animate*\ &order=created_at.desc&limit=30" \ -H "[REDACTED] ``` `ilike.*foo*` is case-insensitive substring. Wrap in `*…*` — the leading/trailing asterisks are SQL `%` wildcards. ### 2. Restrict to high-signal channels ```bash &channel_name=in.(daily_summaries,wan_comfyui,wan_chatter,wan_gens,resources,wan_resources) ``` ### 3. Compose AND across multiple content terms Repeat the `content=ilike.*X*` filter — PostgREST ANDs duplicate keys: ``` &content=ilike.*wan%20animate*&content=ilike.*workflow* ``` ### 4. OR […]

> AGENT

Thought through with three sources — LTX's own docs, the WaveSpeed guide, and Banodoco practitioners actually training LTX. Here's my take. ## Three findings that frame the answer 1. **LTX 2.3's own auto-captioner produces four sections**: visual content, **speech transcription (word-for-word)**, sounds, on-screen text ([Lightricks/LTX-2 dataset-preparation.md](https://github.com/Lightricks/LTX-2/blob/main/packages/ltx-trainer/docs/dataset-preparation.md)). Whatever distribution the base model was trained on, it has seen captions with dialogue baked in. 2. **The official structured-prompting recipe** is Scene / Action / (Music): scene includes "character age, clothing, hair, accessories, surrounds"; action includes camera movement, sounds, **what the character says and their voice tone** ([WaveSpeed guide](https://wavespeed.ai/blog/posts/ltx-2-3-lora-training-guide-2026/)). 3. **Trigger tokens in LTX trainer are a single global flag**, not per-bucket or per-outfit. They get prepended to every caption automatically. Not designed to distinguish character variations ([Lightricks docs](https://github.com/Lightricks/LTX-2/blob/main/packages/ltx-trainer/docs/dataset-preparation.md)). The "trigger word" pattern for distinguishing things is just **repeating the same phrase verbatim** across captions until it becomes a learned identifier. Banodoco practitioner consensus: detailed captions help style LoRAs ([cseti007 in ltx_chatter](https://discord.com/channels/...)), and even very short captions work for control/IC LoRAs (oumoumad: *"a fixed caption like 'clean footage' or probably nothing at all would've still worked"*). Detail level is task-dependent. ## My answer to your three questions ### Q: Should captions contain the actual dialogue/quote? **Yes, include it when available, but don't go scrape Whisper for it.** Two reasons: - LTX 2.3 is an audio+video model and its base captioner emits speech transcription as a first-class section. Including dialogue matches the prior the model was trained on — there's measurable downside to *omitting* what its base distribution expects. - For Seinfeld specifically, **the dialogue rhythm IS the style.** The pauses, the "but it's a *good* naked," the cadence of "what's the deal with…" — if you want the LoRA's *audio* output (LTX 2.3 generates audio) to feel like Seinfeld, dialogue presence matters. But: don't Whisper every clip if it's not already there. The marginal gain on a 30-clip style LoRA isn't worth the orchestration cost. If your VLM caption already heard the audio and put a quote in, great; if not, leave that field empty. ### Q: Brackets/free-text for outfit+hair vs. special tokens for location **Your intuition is right on location, and right on outfit — but for a different reason than you stated. Here's the cleaner framing:** | What | Format | Why | |---|---|---| | Scene/location | **Discrete token** (`jerrys_apt`, `monks_diner`) | Small bounded set (~3), 15+ clips per token, and […]

Saved. Now the corrected recommendation, all-the-way-best-practice version: ## Full LTX-2.3 four-section caption format Per the official Lightricks docs, the base captioner emits **four sections** — train on the same four: ``` Scene: A {shot_type} shot inside {scene_token}. {character_anchor_per_visible_char}. {camera_motion_or_static}. Speech: "{verbatim_dialogue_quote_for_speaker_1}" — {speaker_name}, {tone}. "{verbatim_dialogue_quote_for_speaker_2}" — {speaker_name}, {tone}. (or "No spoken dialogue." when none) Sounds: {laugh_track_clause}. {ambient_sound}. {sfx}. {music_clause_if_any}. On-screen text: {visible_text_or_"None"}. Style: Seinfeld sitcom, 90s NBC multi-cam look, studio sitcom lighting. ``` Concrete example for one of our clips: ``` Scene: A wide shot inside jerrys_apt. George, a short stocky balding man with glasses in a polo shirt and chinos, stands by the door. Jerry, a thin man in his thirties with short dark hair in a button-down shirt and jeans, naps on the couch. Static camera, medium-wide framing. Speech: "Jerry. Jerry, wake up." — George, hushed, urgent. Sounds: Soft apartment ambience, a refrigerator hum. No music. On-screen text: None. Style: Seinfeld sitcom, 90s NBC multi-cam look, studio sitcom lighting. ``` ## What this means for the pipeline | Change | Where | Impact | |---|---|---| | **Add Whisper transcription per accepted clip** | New step in `dataset_build/run.py` after cut, before caption. Reuse existing `builtin.transcribe` (Whisper). | ~+5s per clip, ~$0 (Whisper is local). Output saved next to the clip as `<clip>.transcript.json`. | | **Extend `caption.json` schema to 4 sections** | `astrid/packs/seinfeld/schemas/caption.json` (auto-generated by `vocab_compile.py`). Add `speech_transcription`, `sounds`, `on_screen_text` fields. Speech is structured (per-speaker quote + tone). | More structured output, larger schema, more tokens per caption. | | **Switch from `visual_understand` (frame) to `video_understand` (full clip + audio)** | Caption step in orchestrator. We extended `visual_understand` with `--response-schema` earlier; `video_understand` already supports response schemas natively per its run.py:257. | Captions now describe MOTION across the clip, not a frozen frame. Catches "George *enters* the diner" vs "George stands in the diner." Costs more per call (best-mode video understand) but matches the LoRA's actual training samples. | | **Replace `outfit` enum with stable character anchor phrases** | `vocabulary.yaml` gets a `character_anchor` field per character ("George, a short stocky balding man with glasses, wearing a polo and chinos" / "George, …wearing a windbreaker"). VLM is instructed to use these anchors verbatim. | Repetition turns the anchor into a learned identifier without burning a discrete token slot. | | **Keep discrete tokens for**: `scene`, `shot_type` | Already in vocab. | These are the heavy-lifting tokens — small set, well-distributed, used at […]

> DEVELOPER

Sometimes there's a scene change at the VERY end of a clip, why is this?

> AGENT

Real issue — and a load-bearing one for training quality. **Why it happens:** Scene segmentation is done by `builtin.scenes` (PySceneDetect's `ContentDetector` by default). It compares each frame to the previous and emits a boundary when the visual diff crosses a threshold. The default threshold (~27 on a 0-255 scale) is tuned to be conservative — it catches hard cuts reliably but **misses**: 1. **Soft transitions** — cross-dissolves, fades, match cuts on the same set with similar lighting. The per-frame diff stays under threshold even though a real cut happened. 2. **Cuts to a visually similar shot** — e.g., a wide-to-medium within Jerry's apartment with the same color palette barely trips ContentDetector. 3. **Frame-perfect boundary timing** — even when a cut IS detected, PySceneDetect reports a timestamp that's typically 1-3 frames late. ffmpeg seeks to the nearest keyframe and rounds. The net effect is usually a 100-200ms "tail" of the next shot bleeding into the previous clip. Cumulative result: a clip that *should* end at second 7.5 ends at 7.7, and frames 7.5-7.7 are from the next shot. **Why this matters for LoRA training:** If a clip contains a scene change, the LoRA learns "transitions" as part of the style — exactly what we DON'T want. The training objective tells it "this 8-second video is what jerrys_apt looks like," and if frame 7.8 is suddenly Monk's diner, the LoRA bakes that visual surprise into its representation of jerrys_apt. **Three fixes, escalating effort:** | Fix | Cost | Effect | |---|---|---| | **End-trim safety margin** (cut 0.3s off every clip's end) | one-line ffmpeg change | Catches the 100-200ms "tail" problem for free. Doesn't help with scene-detect false negatives mid-clip. | | **Lower scene threshold** (`--threshold 20` or use `AdaptiveDetector`) | flag change in orchestrator | Catches softer cuts. Side-effect: more false positives → some real scenes split unnecessarily. | | **VLM scene-change verifier** | ~$0.005 per accepted clip | After cutting, ask `video_understand` "does this clip contain a scene change? yes/no." Reject if yes. Highest accuracy, most expensive. | **My recommendation:** all three, layered: 1. Add 0.3s end-trim by default (cheap insurance) 2. Switch to `AdaptiveDetector` or lower ContentDetector threshold for the segmentation step (catches more cuts pre-cutting) 3. Add an optional `--strict-no-scene-change` flag that does the VLM verification step (default on, since this is a real quality issue for LoRAs) This is a real quality bug we should fix before […]

> DEVELOPER

it's a hard cut to a very different scene, is the pyscene thing relaible?

> AGENT

Honest answer: PySceneDetect is **reliable for clean hard cuts on broadcast-quality footage** but is known to struggle on YouTube-compiled material. Three concrete failure modes for our case: 1. **Compilation re-encoding artifacts** — YouTube compilation channels often add a ~10-frame fade-to-black or cross-dissolve at the splice point. ContentDetector sees this as gradual frame-by-frame change that never crosses its absolute-difference threshold, so it doesn't fire. Then the actual new content arrives and triggers a boundary a few frames LATE — so the previous scene's `end` ends up well past the real cut. 2. **Variable framerate / dropped frames** — YouTube re-encodes can produce VFR or "stretched" frames that read as low frame-to-frame difference even across hard cuts. ContentDetector is sensitive to FPS irregularity. PySceneDetect's docs explicitly call this out. 3. **AdaptiveDetector is meaningfully better than ContentDetector for compilations** — it uses a moving-window comparison instead of an absolute threshold, which handles fades and re-encode noise much better. The PySceneDetect maintainers recommend it for "non-broadcast" sources. For our case (YouTube compilation channels), ContentDetector with default threshold is genuinely the wrong choice. Worth verifying empirically before I patch — let me look at one of the broken clips. **Which clip in the reviewer has the trailing cut?** Tell me the clip_id (or roughly which one — "the third monks_diner one" works) and I'll: 1. Read its `scenes.json` and see what PySceneDetect actually reported 2. ffprobe the clip to see if it really has a cut 3. Compare what AdaptiveDetector would have produced That tells us whether to switch detectors, add end-trim, add VLM verification, or all three. Lemme not patch blind.

> DEVELOPER

And is there a way to just like ask, is there a cut in the scene for example and then to remove the part of the clip that has that cut and if so where you know. So like the first second last second you know whatever third second and then just cut it out. What's a good way to approach this just to get reliable shots?

> AGENT

Yes — this is the right instinct, and IMO it should be the default. Here's the shape I'd build: ## The fix: VLM-verify-and-trim Slot a tiny `video_understand` call **between cut and transcribe**, with a focused JSON schema: ```json { "has_cut": bool, "cut_at_s": number | null, // timestamp within the clip "cut_kind": "hard" | "soft_dissolve" | "fade" | null, "confidence": 0-1, "reasoning": "short" } ``` Then act on the result: | Verifier says | Action | |---|---| | `has_cut: false` | Proceed (current pipeline) | | `has_cut: true, cut_at_s: <t>` | Recompute the two sides — `before` = `[start, start+t-margin]`, `after` = `[start+t+margin, end]`. Pick the longer side. Re-cut from the source mp4. If both sides < `--min-scene-s`, reject the clip and free the bucket slot. | | `has_cut: true, cut_at_s: null` | Model saw a cut but can't localize → reject (rare, model has to be very uncertain to do this). | Why this works for our exact failure mode: - Gemini sees frame-by-frame motion AND audio. Hard cuts in YouTube compilations are completely unambiguous to it — it'll get this right close to 100% of the time on the failure cases PySceneDetect misses. - Uses the same `video_understand --response-schema` primitive we already added — zero new infrastructure. - Cost: ~$0.005/clip in fast mode (Gemini Flash is plenty for "is there a cut, where"). 30 clips = $0.15. - Content-addressed: hash includes the verifier result so re-runs skip already-verified clips. ## Embedding the wisdom In CAPTIONING.md (or a sibling `DATASET_QUALITY.md`) — short section explaining: - Why we VLM-verify after PySceneDetect (compilation re-encoding makes ContentDetector miss boundaries) - The hard cuts are unambiguous to video-native VLMs but soft to frame-diff heuristics - The trim-the-longer-side policy + min-duration check - This is a permanent part of the dataset_build pipeline, on by default, off only via `--no-scene-verify` ## For the existing 30 clips One-shot pass: for each cut clip, run the verifier; where cuts are found, re-cut from the source mp4 and re-caption that clip. Maybe 5-8 of the 30 are affected (best guess), so re-caption cost is small. **This is straightforward to build — ~80 LOC change in `dataset_build/run.py` + a new helper + a docs section.** Want me to: 1. Build it 2. Run the verifier on existing 30 clips, trim where needed, re-caption affected ones 3. Relaunch the reviewer Same chain pattern as before, ~30-45 min total. Approve and I'll […]

> AGENT

Solid signal from Banodoco. Here's the synthesis: ## How many clips per bucket | Source | Per-bucket size | Use case | |---|---|---| | **Richard Servello's char-LoRA tool** | "50 clips per character for 200 total" | Multi-character character LoRA — explicit quotas to balance | | **WackyWindsurfer** | "50 images for style LoRAs, now testing 100" | Style LoRA (images, not video) | | **Cseti** | "around 1k video pairs" | IC-LoRA (camera movement — broader) | | **Kijai** | "small 50-clip dataset showed promise" — but flagged overfitting risk at higher step counts | IC-LoRA reference work | | **crinklypaper's 90s anime LoRA** | 12 show triggers, scaled wide | Style LoRA across shows | | **NC17z** | asked about 25 clips for a talking head — no one said "overkill" | Single-subject narrow LoRA | **Where we sit at 15/bucket: well below community floor.** The de facto floor for style LoRAs is ~50 per concept; 100 is the comfortable middle. **15 validates the pipeline; it won't produce a good LoRA.** When we go for real training, scale to ~50/bucket minimum. ## Should we homogenize to one action (e.g., all sitting)? **No — opposite. Variety within the bucket is the goal.** Direct quote from Richard Servello (whose char-LoRA tool is well-known): > "clip selection is also completely random now. So if you drop in a full season of a show and set max samples to say 200 it will randomly sample every single episode to find clips. So you get a really nice variety instead of just 1 episode" The principle (and Kijai echoes this for IC-LoRAs with "reference randomization was the most important change to battle overfitting"): **the LoRA should learn the SCENE/STYLE as the invariant, and learn that ACTIONS/ANGLES vary within that scene.** If we homogenize to "all sitting in Monk's booth," the LoRA bakes "Monk's = sitting" into the representation and will resist prompts like "George storms out of the booth." What you DO want consistent across all clips in a bucket: - The locked scene token (`jerrys_apt`) — every clip from that location, no exceptions - The style suffix (verbatim repetition) - Character anchor phrases (verbatim repetition) What you DO want varied within a bucket: - Shot types (wide, medium, close-up, over-shoulder mix) - Character combinations (Jerry alone vs Jerry+George vs Jerry+George+Elaine) - Actions/motion (sitting, standing, entering, gesturing, talking, laughing) - Camera framing (left/right/center […]

> DEVELOPER

And how many seconds of footage should we be training on? Um, is this too many right now or yeah. Like what do you think? What's the best practice in hive mind?

> AGENT

Real data on both axes. ## Our current footage ``` Total: 268.5s (~4.5 min) across 30 clips jerrys_apt: 15 clips, 133.1s, avg 8.9s monks_diner: 15 clips, 135.4s, avg 9.0s Duration: min 3.8s | p25 7.7s | median 9.1s | p75 10.4s | max 14.1s ``` ## What LTX 2.3 training actually consumes The `1 + 8N` frame-count rule is the hard constraint. At 24 fps, valid clip lengths are: | Frame count | Duration @ 24fps | Who uses it | |---|---|---| | 49 | ~2.0s | T2's IC-LoRAs (the most common bucket) | | 73 | ~3.0s | mid-range | | 97 | ~4.0s | mid-range | | 121 | ~5.0s | crinklypaper's 90s anime style LoRA (the upper bound several practitioners cite as "max") | **Quotes from practitioners:** - **crinklypaper** (style LoRA): *"832x480 max 121 frames for 16:9 and 736x544 max 121 frames for 4:3. And the distribution of frame count per clip is pretty good. Both short and long clips"* → variable length, capped at ~5s - **VRGameDevGirl84**: *"chunk mode splits the source video into separate training samples (33 or 49 frames each)"* → ~1.4–2s per sample - **Alisson Pereira**: *"sometimes 9, sometimes 73, sometimes 121"* → variable is fine - **SmaX**: *"aligning the frame counts was worthwhile (from a concept learning point of view) Musubi breaking those up can mess with the training signal"* → split-by-trainer can hurt - **mdkb**: *"thought 20 seconds was max but then I would have thought frame count matters... 30 sec degrades"* → upper bound where signal collapses ## What this means for our 9-second clips The trainer will not see our 9s clips as 9s. It'll either: - **Crop** a random ~49–121-frame window per clip per epoch - **Subsample** uniformly (every Nth frame to fit 121 frames) - **Chunk** into multiple training samples (49 frames each) — this is what VRGameDevGirl84 mentioned Net effect: **our 9-second clips train more like 3–5-second clips**, with some footage going unused per pass. Not broken — but the captions describe the whole 9s while training sees a slice. ## Three options, with my recommendation | Option | Effect | When to do it | |---|---|---| | **A. Keep as-is** | 30 long clips, trainer subsamples; captions slightly oversell what training sees | NOW (v1 validation run) | | **B. Tighten max to ~5s** in next collection pass | Re-cut existing or set `max-scene-s = […]

> DEVELOPER

But that means the prompt will be misaligned with the actual data though, is that correct?

> AGENT

Yes — that's exactly the right concern, and I was soft on it. The misalignment is real and asymmetric across the four caption sections: | Caption section | Misalignment when trainer subsamples? | Why | |---|---|---| | **Scene** (token, anchors, style suffix) | **No** | These are invariant across the whole clip — same location, same characters, same style. Any 2s window of a one-shot clip still matches. | | **Camera + shot_type** | **No** (assuming the verifier caught all cuts) | Stays constant within a single continuous shot. | | **scene_description** (motion verbs) | **Partial** | "Jerry enters, sits, gestures" → a 2s window might show only "Jerry gestures." Caption oversells what the trainer sees. | | **speech_transcription** (verbatim quotes) | **Yes, and badly** | Caption transcribes ALL 9 seconds of dialogue. A 2s window contains maybe one line out of three. Now the trainer sees one line of dialogue while the caption claims three. The model learns a noisy mapping. | | **Sounds** (laugh track, ambient) | **Partial** | Laugh track might be only in second 6 of a 9s clip. A 2s window from seconds 1-3 has no laugh track but caption claims "present." | **For a pure visual style LoRA the static tokens carry most of the signal, so the misalignment in dialogue/sounds matters less.** But — and this is the part where you're right — **LTX 2.3 generates audio too.** If we want the LoRA to inherit Seinfeld's dialogue rhythm and laugh-track timing (which we explicitly went out of our way to capture in the speech_transcription section), the audio-aligned subset of each clip must match its caption. With 9s clips subsampled to 2s, it doesn't. **The clean fix is what you implied: cut clips to match the trainer bucket size.** Concretely: - Set the orchestrator's `--max-scene-s` to **5.0** (= 121 frames @ 24fps, the maximum LTX 2.3 trainer bucket) - Set `--min-scene-s` to **2.0** (= 49 frames, the smallest standard bucket) - Going forward, every clip lands inside one trainer bucket — captions describe exactly what the trainer sees For the **existing 30 clips**, two paths: | Path | Effect | |---|---| | **Re-trim to 5s max** (cheap, lossy) | Re-cut each long clip from source to its first 5s (or middle 5s); re-caption (~$0.75); lose late-clip dialogue | | **Split long clips into 2-3 sub-clips** (best alignment, more samples) | A 9s clip becomes […]

Smoke test ✓ — pipeline works. 2 parents → 4 sub-clips, each independently captioned with window-local content. **One observation worth flagging before scaling up:** the two windows from the SAME parent clip (`FTsxlM90e70-s10`) gave inconsistent outfit calls for Elaine — w0 said "casual top and jeans," w1 said "blazer and skirt." That's the same 9-second scene split in half. The visual ground truth is the same outfit; the noise is in Gemini's enum-matching when the outfit is borderline (Elaine often wears blazer-with-casual-top combinations that don't fit cleanly into our enum). This is a property of the discrete outfit_variant taxonomy, not a pipeline bug. **Two ways to handle it:** | Path | Effect | |---|---| | Accept the noise | Mild label inconsistency between adjacent windows is probably fine for a style LoRA — the visual data is identical, the trainer sees both, the LoRA averages out | | Add an "ambiguous" / "other_casual" variant per character | Lets the model say "I don't know, picking closest" without forcing a confident pick — cleaner labels | Honest take: this is small enough to defer. The static tokens (scene, anchors, style suffix) are doing most of the LoRA's work; outfit-call noise within one parent is in the noise floor. > AGENT

Yes — that's a real and worthwhile optimization. The verifier is asking a coarse question (binary "is there a cut + roughly where") that doesn't need 24fps video. Sampling at 5fps captures any hard cut perfectly: max gap between sampled frames is 200ms, and a cut produces an unmistakable boundary between adjacent sampled frames. **Concretely the swap is:** | | Current | Proposed | |---|---|---| | Tool | `builtin.video_understand` (Gemini Flash, full clip) | `builtin.visual_understand` (gpt-4o-mini, contact sheet of N sampled frames) | | Input | 5s × 24fps = 120 frames + audio | 5s × 5fps = 25 frames as a contact sheet image | | Cost | ~$0.005/call | ~$0.0005/call (~10× cheaper) | | Wall time | 5–10s per clip | 2–3s per clip (~3–5× faster) | | Accuracy on hard cuts | excellent | excellent (boundary between cells is unmistakable) | | Accuracy on soft dissolves | good | acceptable (might miss a 10-frame dissolve if it falls entirely between samples — rare) | | Audio cues | yes | no (irrelevant for cuts) | **Implementation:** `_verify_clip_clean` swaps to call `visual_understand --video clip.mp4 --at 0.2 --at 0.4 --at 0.6 ...` (auto-generated stride from clip duration) with the same `scene_verify.json` schema. visual_understand already builds the contact sheet automatically and we already extended it with `--response-schema`. ~15 LOC change in the helper. **My recommendation:** **don't restart this rebuild** — it's ~80% done, restarting would cost more time than letting it finish. Implement the optimization NOW so it's ready for: 1. The next iteration scale-up (50/bucket = ~150 verifies = 10× savings = ~$0.50 saved + 5min wall saved) 2. Future projects reusing this pipeline Also a nice property: the optimization further reinforces the principle that **the verifier is a coarse safety gate, not the caption pipeline**. Keeping it cheap means we never have to weigh "should I skip the verifier to save cost" — it's basically free. Want me to: 1. Let the current rebuild finish (~5 more min) and report results 2. Implement the optimization right after (separate, mechanical change, easy to verify on a single test clip) 3. Use it from the next collection-scale-up onward Or do you want me to ship the optimization before the next big run regardless of when?

Subagent launched on the verifier optimization. Now to your bigger question — it's a really good one and it's worth thinking through carefully. ## The "anchor scene" idea — yes, this is a real technique What you're describing is what character-LoRA practitioners call a **hero scene** or **reference scene**: a single chunk of footage where the subject is captured in canonical form (canonical outfit, canonical lighting, canonical age/look, multiple angles). It's used as the *spine* of the dataset, with everything else providing variety around it. **Why it would help us specifically:** The model is trying to learn "what does Kramer look like?" from ~10 clips spread across different episodes, different seasons, different lighting, different wardrobes. Right now our anchor caption phrase says `"Kramer, a tall lanky man with wild hair, wearing a vintage shirt and pleated pants"` — but the underlying pixels show Kramer in maybe 6 different shirts. The model has to average across that, which is what produces the inconsistency you're worried about. If we add a hero scene where all 4 are in jerrys_apt in *one canonical look* with 8-12 angles (close-ups, two-shots, wides) — and a second hero scene in monks_diner — then the model has a strong "this is canonical" signal that the variety footage refines, instead of having to invent its own average. ## The tradeoffs - **Pro:** Much more consistent generations. Each character has one "ground truth" look the model leans on. - **Pro:** Free shot-type variety (close-up + two-shot + wide of the same moment is gold for a LoRA — same subjects, different framings). - **Con:** Risk of overfit to that exact wardrobe. Mitigated by keeping the variety footage in the dataset and making sure anchor captions don't over-specify (e.g., `"Kramer in vintage shirt and pleated pants"` stays a general anchor phrase, not `"Kramer in the red Hawaiian shirt"`). - **Con:** Finding the perfect scene is hard. Most "all-four in apt" scenes are shot mostly wide and don't cycle through close-ups much. The diner is even harder — usually only 2-3 of them at a booth. ## What I'd suggest 1. **Search for candidate hero scenes deliberately** — there are well-known group scenes where all 4 are in Jerry's apt for several minutes (e.g., parts of "The Contest," "The Marine Biologist," various holiday/event episodes). For Monk's diner, "The Soup Nazi" and various 4-person diner conversations work. 2. **Cut them densely** — […]

That's a real false-positive concern — at our scale even a 5% false-positive rate would have rejected 3 good clips. Two ways to compensate: 1. **Raise the confidence threshold** from 0.6 → 0.75 or 0.8. If 4o-mini is uncertain about a subtle change, we trust it less. Raising the threshold won't help; it's all noise that looks like signal to the model. Worse signals: 1. **Run-to-run inconsistent.** s12 flipped from "no cut" (subagent's earlier run) → "hard cut @ 1.77s" (this run) on the *same input*. That's a stability issue, not just a calibration issue. 2. **Confuses cinematography with scene changes.** The s23-w1 reasoning was *"a new character entering the shot"* — that's a normal actor entrance, not a cut. The s12 reasoning was *"camera angle shifts significantly"* — also normal reframing within a single shot. 3. **Always 0.95.** No useful gradient to calibrate against. The contact-sheet + 4o-mini path can't reliably distinguish *new shot* from *new actor in frame* or *camera reframe*. That's exactly the discrimination scene-cut detection needs. ## Verdict **Revert to `video_understand` for verification.** The original cost (~$0.005 × ~60 clips = $0.30 per rebuild) was never a real problem — we optimized prematurely. The video-native VLM had the actual temporal reasoning needed. > DEVELOPER

So And it'll chop the shot into many different subshots, right?

> AGENT

Yes exactly. The 160.83s source block is being chopped into 31 separate ≤5s sub-clips (`w0` through `w30`), each cut from the source mp4 with `_cut_clip`, each independently verified, transcribed, and captioned. If the verifier detects a within-window cut, that sub-clip is either trimmed to the clean half (the 3.85s / 4.25s / 4.55s ones you're seeing) or rejected entirely. So one 160s source scene → up to 31 training samples, each is one continuous shot of ≤5s with its own window-local caption + transcript. That's what scales the dataset.

Human: <task-notification> <task-id>b5jr1tcuu</task-id> <summary>Monitor event: "v3 hero-scene pipeline progress + completion"</summary> <event>[w10] 5.00s ok (29.7s) — Scene: A two_shot shot inside jerrys_apt. Jerry, a thin man in his thirties with short dark hair, wearing a sweater and jeans. Elaine, a wom</event> </task-notification>

> DEVELOPER

And is it being chopped up based on the transcription?

> AGENT

No — chopping is purely time-based, aligned to LTX's `1+8N` frame buckets (so each window is 5s, 4s, 3s, or 2s at 24fps, never more). Transcription happens *after* the cut and just records whatever speech fell inside the window. Dialogue may start or end mid-sentence — that's expected and the caption prompt (`v3_window_local`) tells the captioner to describe only what's in the window, not the surrounding sentence. The cutting hierarchy: 1. **Source video** (e.g., 312s) 2. **PySceneDetect / ffmpeg scene filter** → scene boundaries (e.g., scene 0: 0-160s) 3. **`_split_to_aligned_subclips`** → time-based 5s windows aligned to LTX buckets 4. **VLM verifier** → if a within-window cut is found, trim to the longer clean half 5. **Transcribe + caption** → window-local Transcript is an *output* of the cut, not an input to it.

> TOOL

<task-notification> <task-id>b5jr1tcuu</task-id> <summary>Monitor event: "v3 hero-scene pipeline progress + completion"</summary> <event>[w9] 5.00s ok (33.4s) — Scene: A two_shot shot inside jerrys_apt. Jerry, a thin man in his thirties with short dark hair, wearing a sweater and jeans. Elaine, a wom</event> </task-notification>

> AGENT

Human: But is is okay if the chopping breaks up the dialogue mid-sentence?

> DEVELOPER

uman: But is is okay if the chopping breaks up the dialogue mid-sentence?

> AGENT

Yes — and intentional. Here's why: LTX 2.3 trains on fixed-length frame buckets (49/73/97/121 frames). At inference, you prompt with a 5s clip's worth of caption + audio, and the model produces a 5s clip. There's no "complete sentences" assumption baked into the model — it learns to match the audio waveform to the visual to the caption, regardless of where utterances begin/end. So a clip that contains "...and then I told him—" with the speaker mid-gesture is a perfectly valid training sample. The caption's `speech_transcription` field records exactly that fragment verbatim. At inference, you'd get a 5s output that includes mid-sentence speech with matched visuals, which is what 5s of video naturally contains. The wrong alternative would be to split on sentence boundaries, which would: - Produce variable-length clips not aligned to LTX's frame buckets → trainer mis-subsamples → captions misalign with the actual sampled window (exactly the v0/v1 mistake we just fixed) - Force us to choose between "respect dialogue" and "respect frame math" Frame math wins. The captioner being told `v3_window_local` ensures the caption describes only what's literally in the 5s — so the training signal stays consistent. One thing to watch in the reviewer: if you see captions claiming dialogue the clip's audio doesn't contain, that's a real bug. But truncated mid-sentence speech that the caption accurately records is fine.

> TOOL

> DEVELOPER

why are there so many cuts between scenes inside a single clip?

> AGENT

Because sitcom shot length and our 5s window length collide. **What's happening:** A "scene" in cinematic terms (continuous moment, one location) is built from many **shots** (single camera takes). In Seinfeld dialogue: - Shot of Jerry (~3s) → cut → shot of Elaine (~3s) → cut → wide two-shot (~4s) → cut → close-up of Jerry's reaction (~2s)… So a 60-second "scene" might contain 15-20 shot cuts. **Why our pipeline sees so many:** PySceneDetect's threshold (`scene > 0.3`) is tuned to catch *location/lighting* changes — like apartment → diner. It's deliberately lax on within-set cuts because adjacent shots in the same set share color, lighting, and background, so frame-to-frame diff stays under threshold. That's why we see "scene 0: 0.00-160.83s" reported as one scene — PySceneDetect grouped all those shot cuts inside as one continuous block. When `_split_to_aligned_subclips` chops that 160s block into 32×5s windows, each 5s window randomly slices across shot boundaries. Since average shot length (~3s) is shorter than window length (5s), **most windows span a cut**. That's what you're seeing trimmed/rejected. **Is this a problem?** Not really — the VLM verifier is doing exactly what we built it to do: catching within-window shot changes that PySceneDetect missed, then trimming to the longer clean half. The result is still single-shot training samples. It just looks loud in the log. **Could be improved by:** 1. **Lower ffmpeg's `scene` threshold** from `0.3` to `0.15` or `0.1` so shot cuts get caught upfront, sub-windows align to shot boundaries 2. **Use a shot-aware detector** like Pyscenedetect's adaptive threshold or a frame-similarity ML model 3. **Accept the current "verify + trim"** approach — works correctly, costs ~$0.005 extra per window in verify calls Option 1 is cheap and worth trying. Want me to drop the threshold and rerun, or leave as-is since the output is correct?

> DEVELOPER

> SYSTEM

Base directory for this skill: /Users/user_c042661f/.claude/skills/hivemind # hivemind A read-only PostgREST endpoint exposes the Banodoco Discord message feed. This is community knowledge about video/image generation that you can't get from official docs — workflow tips, model comparisons, settings tweaks, gotchas, links to Kijai/Ablejones/community workflows. ## Endpoint ``` https://[REDACTED]/rest/v1/message_feed ``` Header (anon publishable key, safe to commit): ``` [REDACTED] ``` ## Schema Each row: | field | type | notes | |----------------|---------|------------------------------------------------------------| | `message_id` | bigint | discord snowflake | | `content` | text | message body — what you search | | `author_name` | text | display name; `null` for some bot/system messages | | `channel_name` | text | scope your search by channel (see list below) | | `channel_id` | bigint | rarely needed | | `guild_id` | bigint | always Banodoco | | `reactions` | jsonb | usually `null` — don't rely on it for ranking | | `created_at` | timestamptz | ISO 8601 | ## Channels worth knowing Highest signal: - `daily_summaries` — bot-curated daily roll-ups by **BNDC**. **Start here** for any topic — these are pre-distilled summaries with attributions and Discord links. - `wan_chatter`, `wan_comfyui`, `wan_gens`, `wan_resources` — Wan / Wan Animate - `ltx_chatter`, `ltx_resources`, `ltx_gens`, `ltx_training` — LTX 2.x - `comfyui` — generic ComfyUI tips - `resources` — pinned community resources - `vibecoding` — tooling/dev chatter - `training_control_loras` — LoRA training know-how Lower signal: `chatter`, `nsfw` (yes there's tech advice in there but be selective), `hunyuanvideo`, `qwen-image`, `chroma`, `flux`, `z-image`, `magi`, `ace-step`, `kandinsky-5`, `seedance`, `hunyuanvideo`, `top_gens`, `art_sharing`. ## Power users to watch These names come up repeatedly with authoritative answers — weight their messages higher: - **Kijai** — author of the WanVideoWrapper / most ComfyUI nodes for Wan/LTX. Treat as canonical. - **Ablejones** — context windows, color matching, native Comfy integrations, PRs to ComfyUI core. - **djbfilmz** — heavy Wan Animate user, mocap / reskinning experiments. - **42hub** — curates the [wanx-troopers.github.io](https://wanx-troopers.github.io/) knowledge base. - **BNDC** — the daily-summary bot. ## Query patterns (PostgREST) Always URL-encode spaces (`%20`). Use `Accept: application/json` implicitly. ### 1. Basic substring search ```bash curl -s "https://[REDACTED]/rest/v1/message_feed\ ?select=content,author_name,channel_name,created_at\ &content=ilike.*wan%20animate*\ &order=created_at.desc&limit=30" \ -H "[REDACTED] ``` `ilike.*foo*` is case-insensitive substring. Wrap in `*…*` — the leading/trailing asterisks are SQL `%` wildcards. ### 2. Restrict to high-signal channels ```bash &channel_name=in.(daily_summaries,wan_comfyui,wan_chatter,wan_gens,resources,wan_resources) ``` ### 3. Compose AND across multiple content terms Repeat the `content=ilike.*X*` filter — PostgREST ANDs duplicate keys: ``` &content=ilike.*wan%20animate*&content=ilike.*workflow* ``` ### 4. OR […]

Got everything I need. ## LTX 2.3 long-form / continuation — what hivemind says **TL;DR: Yes, but the canonical workflow isn't merged yet. Two viable paths today.** ### Path 1 — Native context windows (Ablejones' work, in progress) **Ablejones** has been building context-window support for LTX 2.3 in ComfyUI. This is *the* canonical long-video approach — generate a 30s/60s+ clip in one go by sliding overlapping context windows during sampling. - **Branch (testing, not yet merged into main ComfyUI)**: `https://github.com/drozbay/ComfyUI/tree/20260322a_ltx_contextwin_test_combined` - **Key insight from Ablejones (2026-04-27)**: *"Each context window, in the basic implementation, is executed totally independently for each step. The windows are totally unaware of each other only after the blending before the next step. A model like LTX-2 is quite good at taking existing information in the latent from a previous step and extrapolating it, so it works better than Wan."* - **Demonstrated results**: 3+ min videos with **remarkably fluid audio across windows** (despite some visual drift) — daily summary 2026-04-28 - **Tradeoff**: background can drift at the beginning; prompt-relay (different prompts per window) doesn't work yet; long-form is best for stable shots, not heavy scene changes - **Quality hack from Ablejones**: render at *low resolution with no context windows*, then upscale 3-4× *with* context windows + partially-masked audio. Lets you get high-quality long-form without the basic implementation's drift. Also relevant: **`LTX Upscaler 1.1`** ships a fix for the "logo" artifact on long-video upscaling. ### Path 2 — Manual extension via `LTXVImgToVideoInplace` (works today) Per **42hub (2026-05-08)** and **N0NSens (2026-05-06)**: - Use `LTXVImgToVideoInplace` (preferably `LTXVImgToVideoInplaceKJ` from KJNodes — Ablejones flagged a bug in the core node that clobbers noise masks and doesn't clone latents properly) - Feed it the **last `8N+1` frames** of your previous clip as the "start sequence" - Add `LTXV Add Latent Guide` with reference image at index `-8` for character/scene consistency - Generate, append, repeat Pattern: each extension is one new ≤5s window, audio + visuals continue. Works but per-clip, not single-pass. Also useful: `LTXVLoopingSampler` (per **Chandler**, 2026-05-08) — masks and extends a video latent. Original LTX version is video-only; Chandler made a version that handles audio too ("works ok, still improving"). 42hub described it as "still a mystery." ### Path 3 — Lightricks-official lipdub/IC-LoRA + extension If you want clean character-consistent continuation: **Lightricks just shipped a beta `LTX-2.3-22b-IC-LoRA-LipDub`** (HuggingFace: `https://huggingface.co/Lightricks/LTX-2.3-22b-IC-LoRA-LipDub`, Ablejones 2026-05-11) — works with the latest `ComfyUI-LTXVideo` example workflow. For multi-shot with […]

I want to be able to feed in one clip and for it to output the next to keep things consistent

> AGENT

That's exactly what **`LTXVImgToVideoInplaceKJ` + `LTX Add Latent Guide`** is built for. Concrete recipe: ## "Clip in → continuation out" workflow ``` [Clip N video latent (last 8N+1 frames)] ↓ LTXVImgToVideoInplaceKJ ← extracts the seed sequence from clip N ↓ LTX Add Latent Guide ← reference image at index -8 (locks character/scene) ↓ LTXVScheduler + KSampler ← runs sampling ↓ VAE Decode → Clip N+1 ``` The `-8` index is the key trick from hivemind (42hub, 2026-05-10) — places the reference latent 8 frames before the end so the model anchors on it without forcing it into the visible output. ## What you need installed locally 1. **ComfyUI** (recent — last 2-3 months has the right nodes) 2. **`ComfyUI-LTXVideo`** (Lightricks' official nodes) — provides the base LTX 2.3 nodes 3. **`KJNodes`** (Kijai's pack) — provides `LTXVImgToVideoInplaceKJ`, the bug-fixed version that doesn't clobber noise masks 4. **LTX 2.3-22b model** (`Lightricks/LTX-2.3-22b` HF) 5. **Our scene-LoRA** (once trained) loaded after the base model ## Two things you specifically need to decide 1. **How much overlap?** The `8N+1` rule means the seed sequence is at least 9, 17, 25, 33… frames. ~33 frames = ~1.4s of overlap from clip N → smooths the transition but uses more compute. Banodoco practitioners typically use 17-33. 2. **What reference image?** Three options: - **Last frame of clip N** — tightest continuity, can over-anchor (next clip looks like a still extension of the last frame) - **A canonical character "hero" frame** — more consistent character look over many extensions, but the *scene/lighting* may drift - **Both** — last frame at `-8`, hero at `-16` (multi-guide). Best of both, costs a tiny bit of VRAM. ## My suggestion I'd build you a **ComfyUI workflow JSON** that does this end-to-end: - **Input**: path to a video file (the clip you want to extend) - **Output**: the extended clip (clip + extension stitched, plus the standalone extension) - **Knobs**: extension length (frames), reference strategy (last-frame / hero / both), CFG, seed - **Once your scene-LoRA is trained**, drop it into the LoRA slot, no other changes This is concrete: a workflow JSON file you double-click in ComfyUI to load. **Want me to:** - **(a)** Build that workflow JSON now (I can draft it based on hivemind's described patterns) - **(b)** Wait until your scene-LoRA is trained, then build it with the LoRA slot pre-wired - **(c)** Hold — first finish reviewing […]

> AGENT

I'd recommend the **single-shot training + continuation at inference** path, not the with-transitions approach. Here's why: ## Why NOT to include transitions in training The model learns "what does a 5s clip of Seinfeld look like?" If training samples contain cuts, the LoRA learns that **cuts are part of the Seinfeld look** — and will spontaneously inject cuts mid-generation that you can't control. The whole point of our VLM verifier was to *reject* clips with internal cuts for exactly this reason (DATASET_QUALITY.md: *"if frame 7.5 of an 8-second clip is suddenly Monk's diner, the LoRA bakes that visual surprise into `jerrys_apt`"*). You'd lose: - **Within-clip coherence** (which is the model's prior — LTX is trained on continuous shots) - **Controllability** (you can't tell a LoRA *where* to cut) - **Continuation consistency** (continuation workflows assume the input clip is continuous) ## What the single-shot strategy actually gives you Trained on clean single-shot clips, your LoRA learns: - **Character identities** (Kramer = lanky + wild hair + vintage shirt) - **Set identities** (jerrys_apt = wood paneling, kitchen counter, window) - **Cinematography style** (sitcom flat lighting, eye-level camera) - **Shot-type vocabulary** (we have `close_up`, `two_shot`, `over_shoulder`, `wide`, etc. — model learns each one) At inference, you control the cinematography through **captions**, not through training data structure: - Prompt 1: `"close_up of Jerry in jerrys_apt, wearing sweater and jeans..."` - Prompt 2: `"over_shoulder of Jerry and Elaine in jerrys_apt..."` - Prompt 3: `"wide shot of Jerry, Elaine, and Kramer in jerrys_apt..."` Each one generates a clean continuous shot of that type. **You** assemble the cuts in your editor — same as how the actual show is made. ## Where continuation comes in Two distinct things: 1. **Same-shot continuation** (extend a 5s into 10s): `LTXVImgToVideoInplaceKJ` with the last 8N+1 frames as seed. Generates the *same shot* continuing. 2. **Cross-shot consistency** (cut from clip A to clip B, both featuring Kramer wearing the same outfit): the **LoRA itself** carries this. Both clips draw from the same learned Kramer identity, so cuts between them look like Seinfeld editing rather than two different characters. The "consistency" you want across cuts is **character identity persistence**, which is a *LoRA training* concern. The "consistency" you want within a continuing shot is a *continuation workflow* concern. They're solved at different layers. ## Concrete plan Keep doing what we're doing: 1. **Dataset: clean single-shot ≤5s clips, lots of shot types per character.** […]

You're right — I conflated two things that look similar but are actually three separate consistency problems. Let me untangle: ## Three kinds of "consistency" | What's preserved | Mechanism | Example | |---|---|---| | **Identity** (face, hair, wardrobe) | LoRA | Kramer's face looks like Kramer across all clips | | **Within-shot continuity** (frame-to-frame) | Continuation seed (last 8N+1 frames) | A 5s shot extended to 10s — camera keeps rolling | | **Scene-state across cuts** (Kramer is still on the stool) | Reference image at `-8` | Wide → close-up → cut back to wide, Kramer still on stool in same pose | The third one is what you're describing, and **yes, continuation tooling is exactly the right answer for it** — I undersold this. ## Concrete recipe for your stool scenario Generate the scene as 3 shots that share a scene-state reference: 1. **Shot 1 (wide)**: prompt `"wide shot of Kramer sitting on a stool in jerrys_apt..."` → generate. Save the **last frame** as `shot1_last.png`. 2. **Shot 2 (close-up on Jerry)**: prompt for close-up. **No reference needed** — this shot is on Jerry, Kramer isn't visible, so state doesn't matter. LoRA handles Jerry's identity. 3. **Shot 3 (cut back to wide on Kramer)**: prompt `"wide shot of Kramer on the stool in jerrys_apt..."`. Feed `shot1_last.png` into `LTXV Add Latent Guide` at index `-8`. The model uses it as a **state anchor** — Kramer reconstructs in the same pose on the same stool, even though there was an intercut. The trick is that `Add Latent Guide` at `-8` doesn't force the reference into the visible output (it's "8 frames before the end" — invisible padding). It just biases the latent toward what's in the image. So you get the scene-state without literally pasting the still frame. You can chain: shot 3's last frame becomes the reference for shot 5 (next time you cut back to that wide angle). Through the whole scene, Kramer stays on his stool. ## Why the LoRA can't do this alone The LoRA learns categories: "Kramer," "jerrys_apt," "two_shot." It doesn't learn *specific moments* — "Kramer on this specific stool with this specific drink in his hand." That spatial/state information has to come from *inference-time signals* (reference images, continuation seeds, FFLF workflows). The LoRA is the *vocabulary*; the reference images are the *specific sentence*. ## So the dataset answer is still: clean single-shot clips But […]
