What if the data sets like a mix of just straight shots and ones with cuts at the beginning, you know, so maybe like 75% just single shots and 75% with cuts within the first seconds, you know, to a new shot. Would that kind of allow for both? And then we mentioned that cut in the prompt.

> AGENT

Your instinct is reasonable — caption-conditioned cuts work in image diffusion ("a diptych showing X on the left and Y on the right"), so why not video? But three things stack up against it for LTX scene-LoRAs: ## Why mixing cuts into the training set probably hurts more than it helps 1. **Temporal precision in prompts is weak.** Diffusion video models are bad at counting frames from text. A caption saying *"cuts to a wide shot at 1.2s"* doesn't translate into "produce different pixels starting at frame 29." The model learns a fuzzy association like "this prompt → sometimes the scene changes," and the timing becomes uncontrollable. 2. **Sample efficiency is brutal.** To make "cuts" a controllable variable, you don't just need 25% of clips to contain a cut — you need many examples of *each cut type* (close-up→wide, two-shot→OTS, OTS→reverse-OTS, wide→close-up, …). At 39 cut clips ÷ 5 types ≈ 8 examples each, that's well below the signal threshold. The 75% clean clips will steamroll the 25%; cuts become noise the model averages out. 3. **The loss landscape gets ugly.** During training, the trainer samples random 121-frame windows from your clip. If a clip has a cut at frame 29, the trainer is asking the model to predict frame 29 (a new shot) from frames 0-28 (the old shot) — which is fundamentally unpredictable. Models cope by getting blurrier in those windows, which costs you visual fidelity on the clean-shot stuff too. You'd train *down* the quality of the parts you actually want. 4. **The single-shot prior in the base model is doing real work.** LTX 2.3 is trained predominantly on continuous shots. Your LoRA is *adjusting* that prior toward Seinfeld. If your LoRA fights the continuous-shot prior by introducing cuts, you waste training capacity on undoing the base model's helpful assumption. ## The middle ground that *does* work What you actually want is **shot-type variety** — close-ups, wides, two-shots, OTS — captured as the *category* of the clip, not as something the model has to switch into mid-clip. You already have this: - Vocab has `shot_type: close_up | medium | over_shoulder | two_shot | three_shot | wide` - Each clip's caption includes one shot_type token - At inference, prompting `"close_up of Kramer..."` then `"wide shot of Kramer..."` generates two different shots from the same character identity So if you're worried about **cut variety**, the lever isn't "include cuts […]

> DEVELOPER

And so say if I want to continue from a previous shot, what's the best way to do that? Is there like, would it make sense to have an IC lower end that you like feed one image into and it starts to shot. Yeah, with a different shot from the same cut or what do you think makes sense?

> AGENT

Your IC-LoRA idea is exactly the right direction — and it's a real, well-supported pattern in the LTX community (oumoumad's Outpaint IC-LoRA, Lightricks' official LipDub + HDR IC-LoRAs, etc.). But it's a Phase 3 thing. Three layers of "continuation," roughly ordered from cheapest to most ambitious: ## Layer 1 — Just the scene-LoRA + reference image (today, no new training) ``` [Clip A — generated with scene-LoRA] ↓ Extract Clip A's last frame → shot_a_end.png ↓ [Generate Clip B] - Same scene-LoRA loaded - New prompt: "close_up of Jerry in jerrys_apt..." (different shot_type from A) - LTXV Add Latent Guide: shot_a_end.png at index -8 (acts as state anchor) ``` This is what we discussed earlier. **80% of practical "cut to new angle" cases work with this alone** because: - Scene-LoRA carries identity (Kramer's look, jerrys_apt's set) - Reference image carries state (Kramer-on-stool, lighting, props) - New prompt carries the new shot_type (close-up, OTS, wide) No IC-LoRA needed. Just your scene-LoRA + a 1-line workflow change. ## Layer 2 — Multi-shot orchestration workflow (today, ComfyUI) Use **Jonathan's WhatDreamsCost FFLF Custom Audio Workflow** (we surfaced this in hivemind earlier). You define multiple shots in one workflow run: - Shot 1: image + prompt + timestamp - Shot 2: image + prompt + timestamp - Shot 3: image + prompt + timestamp All shots share the LoRA. Output is one continuous video with controlled cuts. Audio drives sync. This is the practical multi-shot rig that ships today; cuts are handled by the workflow, not by training. ## Layer 3 — Train an "angle-pair" IC-LoRA (future work) The IC-LoRA you're describing would be: **given an input clip showing moment M from angle A, generate the same moment M from angle B**. This is the most powerful but most expensive option. **What it solves that Layer 1 doesn't**: - Reliable reverse angles (camera spins around, sees the other side of the room) - Same-moment intercutting where Layer 1's reference image isn't enough - Style of "TV editing" — over-the-shoulder ↔ reverse-OTS pairs, push-in/pull-out, etc. **Data you'd need**: pairs of clips from the same scene. Seinfeld is *perfect* for this — every conversation scene contains 4-10 shots of the same moment from different angles. We can extract these pairs from the sources we already have. Roughly: - Take each PySceneDetect "scene" (which actually contains many shots of one moment) - Find pairs of adjacent windows that […]

> AGENT

Yes, **Option A (IC-LoRA on shot-pairs)** is the right direction and it's a legit thing to build. **Option B (continuation that cuts)** would fight LTX's prior — continuation is trained to keep the camera rolling, not to inject a cut at frame 0. ## Why an angle-pair IC-LoRA is the right shape Training task is clean and tractable: ``` INPUT: (image_A, caption_B) ↑ ↑ last frame of shot A prompt describing the new shot ("close_up of Kramer in jerrys_apt...") TARGET: video_B (5s of the new angle, same moment, same character state) ``` The model learns the mapping "given this image of moment M, generate a 5s clip that picks up the same moment from a different angle, in a way that respects who-was-where-doing-what." **Why this works much better than B**: - LTX 2.3's continuation primitive is built for *temporal* continuation (next 5s of the same shot). You'd be repurposing it for *spatial* continuation (different angle of same moment), which is a different problem. - The IC-LoRA explicitly trains for the spatial-continuation transformation. The model learns "image_A → video_B" as one cohesive operation, not as "extend then cut." ## Data is already in our sources Every "scene" in PySceneDetect's output (cinematic sense — one continuous conversation) contains 4-10 sub-shots of the same moment. So from each Seinfeld source we already have, we extract pairs: ``` For each cinematic scene S: For each adjacent shot pair (A, B) within S: pair = { "input_image": last_frame(A), "caption": describe(B), // includes shot_type token "target_video": B, } ``` Roughly 80-150 pairs from our 3 sources. Lightricks publishes IC-LoRA training scripts in their repo (cseti007 referenced this in hivemind). ## How it stacks at inference The two LoRAs work together: 1. **Scene-LoRA** (already planned) — loaded at the base model layer. Provides identity, set, style. 2. **Angle-pair IC-LoRA** — loaded on top. Provides "image → new-angle-video" capability. At inference: ``` Load: LTX 2.3 base + scene-LoRA + angle-pair-IC-LoRA Input image: last frame of prior shot Prompt: "close_up of Kramer in jerrys_apt, wearing vintage shirt and pleated pants" Output: 5s clip of Kramer from a new angle, in the same state he was in the prior shot ``` This is much more reliable than the Layer 1 "single reference at index -8" trick because the IC-LoRA was *trained* to do this transformation, not coaxed into it via reference biasing. ## When to actually build it Same staging […]

Let me hit hivemind for IC-LoRA training details before designing the data prep.

> AGENT

Looked it up — IC-LoRAs absolutely take single-frame references, and your Seinfeld-tight intuition is correct: small, specialized datasets converge. Here's the picture from hivemind: ## How IC-LoRA training actually works (per Kijai, zlikwid, oumoumad) **Data structure** is a 4-tuple per sample: - `video_guide` — input video (or extracted from reference image, depending on task) - `reference_image` — single still that anchors what to preserve - `caption` — describes the target - `target_video` — what the model should generate **Critical findings from community**: - **Rank 32 outperforms rank 64** (Kijai — 64 overfits, "arms appearing from ears") - **LR 2e-5 @ 2000 steps** or **5e-5 @ 1500 steps** — both work - **Dataset augmentation on the reference side was THE main win**, more than hyperparameter tuning - **Captions can be minimal** (oumoumad: *"a fixed caption like 'clean footage' or probably nothing at all would've still worked"*) - **Matching dimensions** between reference and target are mandatory - Tools: **ai-toolkit** is the main IC-LoRA training rig (LTX's own docs cover IC-LoRA training too) **Small datasets do converge**: - crinklypaper's motion-blur IC-LoRA: **26 video pairs**, decent results by ~8,500 steps - oumoumad's Outpaint IC-LoRA: 111 videos, 324 references - zlikwid's upscale IC-LoRA: 500 pairs at rank 32 Our ~80-150 angle-pair samples lands squarely in this range. Should converge. ## What our `dataset_build` pack needs to add Conceptually one new step, easy to bolt on: ``` For each (bucket, video_id, scene_idx) in current strict dataset: Find consecutive clips (sorted by start_s in source). For each adjacent pair (clip_A, clip_B) where B immediately follows A: Extract last frame of clip_A.mp4 → clip_B.previous_frame.jpg Pair: (previous_frame.jpg, clip_B.mp4, clip_B.caption.json) ``` That's it. Files we'd add to `accepted/<bucket>/`: - `<id>.previous_frame.jpg` — extracted last frame of the chronologically prior accepted clip - New `pairs.manifest.json` at the top level with the pair list (just a join over existing state) **ffmpeg one-liner per clip**: `ffmpeg -sseof -0.04 -i clip_A.mp4 -frames:v 1 last_frame.jpg` No new VLM calls. No new transcription. Captions stay window-local for clip_B, exactly what we already produce. Implementation: ~60 lines of Python on top of `dataset_build/run.py`. I'd add it as a new function `extract_pairs_step()` that runs after the main pipeline. ## Convergence estimate for a Seinfeld angle-pair IC-LoRA Educated guess based on what crinklypaper and oumoumad reported: | Setup | Steps | GPU hours | Cost (A100) | |---|---|---|---| | ~100 pairs, rank 32, LR 2e-5 | 5k-10k | 3-6 hrs […]

Reviewer open: **http://127.0.0.1:52975/?[REDACTED]** ## v3 strict + pairs — final numbers **Single-clip dataset (scene-LoRA training):** | Bucket | Clips | |---|---| | jerrys_apt | 101 | | monks_diner | 30 | | **Total** | **131** | Rejected: 28 for in-window cuts (correct behavior), 30 transcribe/caption failures. **Pair dataset (IC-LoRA training):** | Pair type | Count | Use for | |---|---|---| | **intra-scene** (same PySceneDetect scene_idx) | 33 | angle-pair IC-LoRA — different shots of the same moment | | **cross-scene** (different scene_idx) | 95 | transition IC-LoRA — cinematic cuts between moments | | **Total pairs** | **128** | | 3 clips are "first of source" so have no prior frame → 131 clips → 128 pairs. ## What's on disk ``` runs/seinfeld-dataset/ ├── accepted/<bucket>/ │ ├── <id>.mp4 ← clip │ ├── <id>.caption.json ← caption + transcript │ └── <id>.previous_frame.jpg ← prior clip's last frame (IC-LoRA reference) ├── state.json ├── provisional.manifest.json ← single-clip manifest (scene-LoRA training) ├── pairs.manifest.json ← pair manifest (IC-LoRA training, filterable by intra_scene) ├── archive-v2-variety/ ← preserved └── archive-v3-trim/ ← preserved ``` Two manifests, three LoRAs possible: - Train scene-LoRA on `provisional.manifest.json` - Train angle-pair IC-LoRA on `pairs.manifest.json` filtered to `intra_scene=true` - Train transition IC-LoRA on `pairs.manifest.json` filtered to `intra_scene=false` Documentation updated in `DATASET_QUALITY.md` with the multi-LoRA architecture and per-LoRA-type data requirements.

And for the IC LoRa and data set, how should that work? And what should that be like?

> AGENT

Here's the shape I'd build toward, grounded in what hivemind says actually works (Kijai, oumoumad, crinklypaper, zlikwid): ## The training-sample shape IC-LoRA tools expect Per ai-toolkit / Lightricks' IC-LoRA training docs, each sample is a small directory or a manifest entry with: ``` sample/ target.mp4 # the 5s clip to generate reference.jpg # the conditioning image, same WxH as target caption.txt # plain text, minimal — what to generate ``` Or — more common in modern toolkits — a JSONL manifest where each row points at the three: ```jsonl {"target_video": ".../clip.mp4", "reference_image": ".../ref.jpg", "caption": "<text>", "tags": {...}} ``` Our `pairs.manifest.json` is already in that shape (just JSON not JSONL). The reference images are extracted at the target's resolution (we did that) — which satisfies the dimension-match requirement Kijai flagged. ## What our current pair dataset gives us, per LoRA type **Angle-pair IC-LoRA** (target use: image of moment M → 5s of same moment from new angle): - Filter `pairs.manifest.json` to `intra_scene=true` → **33 pairs** - Caption = our existing window-local caption (describes the target shot type, characters, action) - Reference = last frame of immediately-prior shot, same conversation moment - Above the 26-pair floor that crinklypaper hit working results on **Transition IC-LoRA** (target use: smooth cut from clip N to clip N+1 of a different moment): - Filter to `intra_scene=false` → **95 pairs** - Same caption shape - More noise in this set because cross-scene means "what came before isn't directly related," which is actually what transition training wants **Scene-LoRA** uses the same clips without the reference image — `provisional.manifest.json` filtered to single-clip mode. So one dataset, three LoRA-shaped views over it. ## Three things our pair data is missing that hivemind says matter ### 1. Reference-side augmentation (Kijai: "the main win, more than hyperparam tuning") Right now each pair has exactly ONE reference image. The biggest IC-LoRA quality lever according to Kijai is producing multiple augmented variants per pair: - **Time variants**: not just the last frame, but `T-0.2s`, `T-0.5s`, `T-1.0s` from the prior clip. Teaches the model that "any frame near the cut" works as conditioning. - **Light spatial augmentation**: center-crop 90%, slight brightness/contrast jitter. NOT flips (would mirror Jerry's face). - **Multiple references per target**: 3-5 variants per target clip, all pointing at it. This roughly 4-5×s the effective training set without collecting more clips. For us: 33 angle-pair × 4 variants = 132 effective samples. ### […]

The HDR / LipDub / Upscale IC-LoRAs all use this exact graph; only the LoRA weights and prompt differ. ### Where 42hub clarified the conditioning nodes (validated) From hivemind (2026-05-05): - **`LTXV Add Latent Guide` with `index=-1`** → reference relevant to all frames (the IC-LoRA conditioning slot) - **`LTXVAddGuide` with `frame_index=-1`** → reference relevant to the last frame specifically - **Training toolkits don't yet support these "proper" reference slots fully** — IC-LoRA trainers use creative workarounds (reference in 1st frame, initial 8 frames, side-by-side panels) So at inference time, the LoRA you trained will work with whichever convention you trained it on. We need to mirror the training-time reference placement at inference. Most modern IC-LoRAs use `frame_index=-1` or initial-8-frames placement. ### Important caveats hivemind raised (worth knowing before we build this) **1. `reference_downscale` parameter must match training and inference** (Kijai community finding, 2026-05-04). If we train with reference downscaled to 1280×704, we must inference with the same. The pack should record this in `pairs.manifest.json` so the inference workflow can read it. **2. I2V mechanism may outperform IC-LoRA for reference following** (mamad8, 2026-05-05): > *"the i2v capability is much stronger than the ic-lora guidance for reference following"* This is a real consideration. Short answer: **no, not formally**. There's no registered Astrid project for the Seinfeld work. Here's the state: ## What exists Astrid has a project model in `astrid/core/project/` (project.json + sources/ + runs/ schema, validators, CLI). The schema docstring says it's for the "local provenance cache (sources/, runs/, project.json)" — i.e., tracking durable creative work. **But no directory in `runs/` actually uses it.** I checked — zero `project.json` files. Including `runs/seinfeld-dataset/`. The seinfeld pack writes via the orchestrator's `--out DIR` flag and treats the output dir as a plain working folder, not a registered project. ## What `runs/seinfeld-dataset/` actually is ``` runs/seinfeld-dataset/ ← plain pack-output directory ├── accepted/<bucket>/... ← clip artifacts + caption.json + previous_frame.jpg ├── candidates/<video_id>/source.mp4 ← downloaded sources ├── state.json ← pipeline-local state (NOT Astrid project schema) ├── provisional.manifest.json ← single-clip manifest ├── pairs.manifest.json ← IC-LoRA pair manifest ├── archive-v2-variety/ ← prior dataset versions └── archive-v3-trim/ ``` The `state.json` here is the **dataset_build orchestrator's** state file, not Astrid's project schema. Different shape entirely. The other entries in `runs/` (`animate-image-001`, `artagents_logo_v5_grid`, etc.) are the same shape — plain output dirs from one-off pack runs. None of them register as Astrid projects either. The project system is built but not wired through the packs. ## Whether to wire it up Pros of formalizing this as an Astrid project: - Provenance tracking (what sources produced what clips) - Discoverability via `astrid.core.project.cli` - Consistency with whatever future workflow the project system enables (the schema comment hints at sprint-2's "first-class timeline containers") Cons: - The current pack-output convention already works - Adding a `project.json` here means coupling the seinfeld pack to the Astrid project model — which is mid-evolution (sprint-3 stash, sprint-4 branch, schema migration in progress) - Probably premature given how often the project model is changing in the sprints **My take**: leave the dataset_build pack as pack-output-dir for now. When the project system stabilizes (post-sprint-X), wrap the seinfeld dataset as a proper project then — at that point the project metadata becomes "name=seinfeld, sources=[BYg, uEv, VMpl], runs=[v2-variety, v3-trim, v3-strict]" which is useful for switching between dataset variants without juggling archive directories. Worth setting up now? Probably not — the active sprint work is reshaping the project schema anyway. Better to wait one or two sprints and adopt the stable shape.

> DEVELOPER

And for the inference is what's happening that we're basically providing a single image and then it's producing a video of the next shot. Is that correct? Based on our prompts.

> AGENT

Yes, exactly. Concretely: **Inputs:** - **1 image** — typically the last frame of the prior clip, but could be any still showing the scene state you want preserved - **1 text prompt** — describes the new shot (e.g. `"close_up of Kramer in jerrys_apt, wearing vintage shirt and pleated pants"`) - **Loaded LoRAs** — scene-LoRA (identity, style, vocab) + angle-pair IC-LoRA (image→new-angle behavior) **Output:** - **1 video, 5s, 24fps** (the LTX bucket size, 121 frames) showing the new shot **The roles:** - The **image** carries scene state — Kramer is on the stool, lighting is X, props are Y - The **prompt** carries the choice of new angle/shot-type — "close_up" not "wide", on Kramer not Elaine - The **scene-LoRA** carries identity — Kramer's face, jerrys_apt's set - The **angle-pair IC-LoRA** carries the transformation — "given image, produce a new-angle 5s clip respecting the conditioning" **To chain into a multi-shot scene:** 1. Generate shot 1 (could be from scratch with scene-LoRA, or from a reference) 2. Extract shot 1's last frame 3. Generate shot 2: feed in shot 1's last frame + new prompt → get shot 2 (5s of new angle) 4. Extract shot 2's last frame 5. Generate shot 3: feed in shot 2's last frame + new prompt → get shot 3 6. ...etc Each step is one image-in, one video-out. You sequence them in your editor or with a multi-shot workflow node. The IC-LoRA's job is making sure the image-conditioning actually controls the output reliably — that "Kramer-on-stool in the reference" becomes "Kramer-on-stool in the new shot" rather than "Kramer somewhere else in a new pose." One subtle thing per hivemind (mamad8): the base LTX 2.3 model **already** has an i2v capability that does something similar — and right now that capability may be stronger at reference-following than custom IC-LoRAs. So the IC-LoRA's incremental value over plain i2v+scene-LoRA isn't guaranteed; that's why I'd recommend testing the i2v path first before training the IC-LoRA.

We don't modify it. The image is `ostris/ai-toolkit:latest` (or a versioned tag — we pin one). No custom template needed — just pass `--image` to our existing executor and the right `ports` config. Caveat to verify on first launch: confirm Ostris's image starts the UI server on container boot, and which port (8675 or 8888). If it requires manual start, our launch script handles it. ### 3. New pack: `astrid/packs/seinfeld/training/` ``` training/ ├── ai_toolkit/ # submodule ├── config_template.yaml # ai-toolkit job config with hivemind defaults ├── generate_config.py # fill template from provisional.manifest.json ├── launch.py # the orchestrator command ├── remote_bootstrap.sh # runs on pod: start UI, point to dataset └── README.md ``` `launch.py` wraps `external.runpod.session` with these […]

There's clear guidance in `AGENTS.md` — and applying it cleanly to your question changes my recommendation. ## Astrid's abstraction model (from AGENTS.md) Three kinds of beings: - **Executors** — one concrete unit of work. Single `run.py` + `executor.yaml` + `STAGE.md`. - **Orchestrators** — compose executors (and other orchestrators) into workflows. - **Elements** — reusable render building blocks (not relevant here). Two iron rules: 1. **Build order: compose existing → create missing executors → only then write the orchestrator.** "Skipping step 1 to write a god-orchestrator that bakes in network calls and business logic is the anti-pattern." 2. **Orchestrators may call orchestrators. Executors must not.** Packs are top-level groupings: `builtin/`, `external/`, `iteration/`, `local/`, `seinfeld/`, `upload/`. `external/` is for external services (where `runpod` already lives). ## Applied to seinfeld training — the right cut Not all in `astrid/packs/seinfeld/`. Some debate whether it should be an executor or an orchestrator — see below. **Submodule** lives at `astrid/packs/external/ai_toolkit/upstream/` (the new pack owns its vendored dep). Base directory for this skill: /Users/user_c042661f/.claude/skills/megaplan-rubric # Megaplan rubric — three questions, three dials Every piece of work answered with three questions. Each question points at one dial. The dials are **independent** — work through each one ignoring the others — then **weigh the three together** holistically to land on a coherent judgment. | | Question | Dial | What it scales | Flag | |---|---|---|---|---| | 1 | What level of raw capability does this need? | **Intelligence tier** | `$/call` | `--profile` | | 2 | What level of process rigor does this need? | **Planning complexity** | `# of calls` | `--robustness` | | 3 | How deeply does each model need to think? | **Depth** | `tokens/call` | `--depth` (with `--phase-model` as the surgical escape hatch) | The dials aren't a mechanical lookup; they're three lenses you apply to the same task, and the answers should fit each other. A high tier with low robustness is usually a mismatch; so is a low tier with `max` depth. When the three feel like they're pulling in opposite directions, the work probably needs to be split. **The dials measure residual complexity, not nominal scope.** Two factors shape residual complexity, pointing in opposite directions: - **Decisions already made decrease it.** A brief where the major design decisions are resolved — architecture chosen, interfaces specified, edge cases enumerated, trade-offs decided — is less complex than the same scope arriving open-ended. The pre-resolved decisions don't disappear; they're paid for upstream by whoever wrote the brief. - **Unknowns remaining increase it.** Even when many decisions are locked, unresolved external API behavior, unmeasured performance characteristics, ambiguous integration targets, or libraries the team hasn't surveyed yet all add complexity the run has to budget for. (That's what `--with-prep` is built for — see below.) When picking a tier, **discount for decisions made and add for unknowns remaining**. A spec-shaped brief with everything known lands a tier lower than the same nominal scope arriving as a sketch; a tightly-defined brief carrying significant unknowns may need either `--with-prep` or a higher tier. **Tightening the brief beats picking a higher tier** — and is often the cheapest way to bring the rubric down a notch. **Defaults to keep in mind:** `--robustness standard`, `--depth` unset (which means the profile's existing depths win — usually `:low` on premium phases), vendor from your config (`claude` if unset). Reach past those […]

It writes `.gitmodules` (tracked), updates the git index, and lands in the resulting diff like any other change. Reviewers re-checking out the branch run `git submodule update --init --recursive` once — same as any submodule. The two real considerations are: 1. **Network access during execute.** The submodule add fetches from GitHub. Megaplan's execute phase runs with network unless explicitly sandboxed — `external.runpod.*` executors already run with `network: true`, so the megaplan setup clearly allows it. Non-issue. 2. **Worktree vs. main checkout.** If you're running megaplan with `--worktree` (isolated branch), the submodule lands in that worktree's `.gitmodules` and merges into main with the rest of the diff. Honest answer, with the caveats: ## Hooked up to RunPod? — Structurally yes, live-tested no **What megaplan will deliver:** - The orchestrator calls `external.runpod.provision → exec → teardown` directly. Network volume threaded through via `--storage-name`. UI port forwarding wired in. Code-level: yes, it's hooked up. **What megaplan will explicitly NOT verify:** - A live training run on a real pod. The brief calls this out as out-of-scope: "Acceptance is structural — verifying the end-to-end run works against a live pod is a separate manual step the human performs." - That means: when megaplan finishes, the **dry-run** path is tested (no pod, just config generation + pre-flight validation), but the first real `--smoke` run is when you'd find out if Ostris's image actually starts the UI on the port we think it does, etc. ## Way to load the data? — Yes, two modes already designed **Dataset state**: 133 MB, 131 clips at `runs/seinfeld-dataset/accepted/`. Small enough that even a fresh upload takes ~30s on a decent connection. **Two paths the orchestrator supports:** 1. **Ephemeral container disk** (default first run): orchestrator `rsync`s the dataset to `/workspace/dataset/` on the pod via `external.runpod.exec`'s `upload_mode=sftp_walk`. Lost when pod tears down. 2. **Network volume** (`--storage-name seinfeld-dataset`): the brief specifies using `astrid/core/runpod/storage.py:ensure_storage()` to find-or-create a persistent volume on first run. After that, the dataset stays mounted on the volume across all future runs — no re-uploading. The brief defaults to the network volume because it makes iteration cheap. ## What you'll need before kicking off the first real run | Thing | Status | How to get it | |---|---|---| | `RUNPOD_API_KEY` env var | **Not set in this shell** (you have it elsewhere — other megaplan runs are sourcing it from `runpod-lifecycle/.env`) | `set -a && source /Users/user_c042661f/Documents/reigh-workspace/runpod-lifecycle/.env && set +a` before invoking, or add to your shell init | | RunPod credit | Unknown | Check at runpod.io console; $30 buffer is comfortable for sprint 1 | | Datacenter choice | Brief doesn't fix one | `--datacenter-id US-CA-2` or similar; needed first time so `ensure_storage` can create the volume | | Ostris image works as expected | **Unverified — prep should confirm** | Once megaplan finishes, the first `--smoke` run is the verification | ## So the realistic flow after megaplan finishes 1. Megaplan finishes → review the diff → merge to your working branch. 2. `set -a && source .../runpod-lifecycle/.env && set +a` 3. […]

I sorted by what'll actually bite vs. nice-to-haves. ## Blockers — will make the live run fail if missed | Gap | Why it matters | |---|---| | **`HF_TOKEN` not in the brief** | LTX 2.3 weights live on HuggingFace and require a token (Lightricks gates the model). AI Toolkit will fail at model-download time without it. | | **Image tag not pinned** | Brief says `ostris/ai-toolkit:latest` — that's reproducibility quicksand. Should pin to a specific tag (or digest) chosen during prep. | | **Datacenter default** | First run needs one to let `ensure_storage()` create the network volume. Brief leaves it as "user supplies"; many DCs don't have the GPU we want. Should bake a sensible default with a fallback list. | | **Crash detection during training** | AI Toolkit can OOM, NaN-out, or hit Gemma3-OOM (per hivemind: `metaphysician`'s issue). If the remote process dies, the orchestrator should detect it (log scan + exit code) and tear down the pod cleanly. Right now the brief assumes training completes. | ## Operational quality — won't block but will frustrate | Gap | Why it matters | |---|---| | **`.env` auto-loading** | You just hit this: `RUNPOD_API_KEY` not in shell. Orchestrator should auto-source a known `.env` location (e.g., `runpod-lifecycle/.env` or `.env.local`) before failing, and print a clear path when it can't. | | **UI URL written to file, not just stdout** | Close the terminal, lose the URL. Should write `<out>/ui_url.txt`. | | **Total cost summary at end** | Each step writes `cost.json`. The orchestrator should sum them and print "Total spend: $X". | | **Concurrent-run guard / Ctrl-C cleanup** | Brief doesn't say what happens if user invokes the orchestrator twice, or hits Ctrl-C mid-run. The `external.runpod.session` executor has try/finally; `provision+exec+teardown` separately doesn't have an equivalent built-in safety net. The orchestrator needs its own. | | **Trigger token convention** | Brief says "minimal captions". But many LoRA conventions use a trigger like `seinfeld scene` prepended. Either: pick a convention (recommend trigger + free-form caption) or explicitly document "no trigger". Affects training quality and inference UX downstream. | | **Reproducibility seed** | AI Toolkit takes a `seed` config. Brief doesn't set one. Should default to a fixed seed (e.g., 42) so runs are reproducible. | ## Polish — defer if not folded in | Gap | Why it matters | |---|---| | **Checkpoint download selectivity** | 8 checkpoints × […]

> DEVELOPER

Are there any tools that could be useful for us to sweep different options that we should add to runpod lifecyle?

> AGENT

Yes, several would be genuinely useful — three we'd actually hit: ## 1. `runpod-lifecycle probe` — "what config will actually launch right now?" Given constraints (`--min-memory 48`, `--max-disk 200`, `--ports 8675/http,22/tcp`, `--has-volume Training` optional), query RunPod's `gpuTypes` + `lowestPrice` + availability and return a ranked list of viable specs by price. No pod created. ```bash runpod-lifecycle probe --min-memory 48 --max-disk 200 --ports 8675/http --json # → # [ # {"gpu_type": "NVIDIA RTX 6000 Ada Generation", "datacenters": ["US-CA-2","US-IL-1"], "price": 0.77, "ram_tier_available": [48,32]}, # {"gpu_type": "NVIDIA L40S", "datacenters": ["EU-RO-1"], "price": 0.86, ...}, # ... # ] ``` This is the single biggest win. We'd have caught the EU-RO-1 congestion in one call instead of five failed provisions. ## 2. Auto-fallback in `launch()` — accept a list of GPU types ```python RunPodConfig(gpu_type=["NVIDIA RTX 6000 Ada Generation", "NVIDIA L40S", "NVIDIA A6000"], ...) ``` Lifecycle iterates in order until one provisions. Today you have to wrap that yourself. Pairs naturally with `probe`: `probe` returns the ranked list, `launch` walks it. ## 3. Wire `--probe-only` / `--dry-run` into the existing CLI verb `runpod-lifecycle provision --probe-only --gpu-type "NVIDIA RTX 6000 Ada Generation"` would query availability + simulate pod-create payload + report "would succeed in DCs [X,Y]" or "would fail because: …" — without actually creating. The current code already validates GPU type + storage existence; bolt the rest on. ## Nice-to-haves but lower priority - **`runpod-lifecycle volumes`** — already wrapped Astrid-side in `astrid/core/runpod/storage.py:list_volumes()`. Could lift to lifecycle so it's reusable. - **Honor `RUNPOD_STORAGE_NAME=""` as "no storage"** — currently empty string still falls through to default; small bug fix in the env-parsing. - **`probe` output exposed as a Python helper** — `find_viable_configs(...)` callable from Astrid's orchestrators directly without subprocess. ## What I'd build first `probe` alone covers ~80% of our pain. The auto-fallback in `launch()` is a nice bow on top, but a caller (Astrid orchestrator) calling `probe` first and picking the top result accomplishes the same thing. If you want, I can sketch a brief for "add `probe` to runpod-lifecycle" as the next sprint after this one lands — different scope, cleaner runpod-lifecycle PR, and would prevent this whole afternoon's pain on future Seinfeld runs.

The `openai/codex-plugin-cc` Claude Code plugin wraps it as `/codex:review`, `/codex:adversarial-review`, etc. — but the underlying invocation is `codex exec`, which you can call directly from the Bash tool. ## When to use Codex - **Second-opinion root-cause analysis.** Cascade of bugs, already explored by Claude — get Codex to look at the same evidence and rank root causes independently. - **Adversarial code review.** Before shipping a non-trivial change, have Codex try to break it. - **Delegated implementation.** When Claude Code has mapped out the fix but the user wants another model to do the writing (and Claude to verify). - **Cross-checking Claude's diagnosis.** Especially when user asks "are you sure?" — Codex is a genuinely independent take, not an echo. Don't use Codex for tasks where you already have the answer — it's not faster, and it's not free (contributes to the user's OpenAI quota). ## Model selection Default to calling `codex exec` with **no `-m` flag** — the user's `~/.codex/config.toml` already pins the model. If you need to override, read the exact model string from that config file first. ## Basic invocation (non-interactive) ```bash codex exec --sandbox read-only "PROMPT_HERE" ``` - `--sandbox read-only` — safe for analysis tasks (no file writes) - `--sandbox workspace-write` — lets Codex modify files in the current project - `--sandbox danger-full-access` — full shell access (avoid unless explicitly requested) - `-m gpt-5-5.4` — pin the model explicitly - `-c key=value` — one-off config override - Output goes to stdout; the final response is the tail after Codex's own trace output For a long prompt, write it to a file first and `cat` it in: ```bash cat > /tmp/codex_prompt.md <<'EOF' ...your prompt here... EOF codex exec --sandbox read-only "$(cat /tmp/codex_prompt.md)" 2>&1 | tail -200 ``` ## Running in background Long Codex sessions (multi-file implementations, deep analysis) should be backgrounded via the `run_in_background` parameter of the Bash tool. Tail the output file when the notification arrives: ```bash codex exec --sandbox workspace-write "$(cat /tmp/codex_prompt.md)" # set run_in_background: true in the Bash tool call ``` ## Writing effective Codex prompts Codex starts with zero conversation context. Like any subagent, the prompt must be self-contained: - Name the working directory explicitly. - List the exact files/paths to read, not "the relevant files." - State the desired […]

> AGENT

OK, hitting the brakes. The "real training" run finished — but not the way we want. Here's what actually happened: | stage | status | |---|---| | provision | ✓ pod up, RTX 6000 Ada | | stage (config + bootstrap + dataset upload) | ✓ all 131 clips uploaded | | **train** | **✗ failed in 11 sec, exit 1, no captured output** | | (pod died around 10 min later — RunPod says "Pod not found") | | eval_grid | ✗ pod gone — uploaded 800+ files of cwd then died | | (orchestrator wrote "HUMAN GATE" anyway — sad path) | Multiple bugs I found from the post-mortem (independent of why pod died): 1. **HF_TOKEN never reaches the pod.** Provision builds `RunPodConfig` without `env_vars`. Ai-toolkit's `ltx2.py` reads `HF_TOKEN` from `os.getenv` and dies without it (LTX-2.3 weights are gated on HF). Almost certainly the train-step failure cause. 2. **Model id is wrong.** Config has `name_or_path: ltx-2.3`. Ai-toolkit's `convert_ltx2_to_diffusers.py` uses `Lightricks/LTX-2.3`. Should be the HF repo id. 3. **Train stdout invisible.** Codex C changed train_cmd from `| tee` to `--log <file>`. The file is on the pod we can't read after pod dies. Should be `2>&1 | tee` so cmd_exec's `exec_result.json` captures it. 4. **`aitoolkit_train` doesn't detect remote failures.** It checks `rv.returncode` (the cmd_exec wrapper, always 0) instead of `exec_result.json["returncode"]`. Said "0 checkpoints → ok" despite the train command exiting 1. 5. **`lora_eval_grid` uploads `Path.cwd()`** (same bug as #4 in aitoolkit_train, different executor I didn't patch). 6. **Pod died.** Reason unclear — could be RunPod-side eviction after the training crash, could be the npm UI process crashing on its own. Not enough data. I think the right move is to pause the autopilot and step through this manually first — provision a pod, SSH in, run `python3 run.py /workspace/config.yaml` by hand and watch the error live. That gives us the ground truth on items 1+2+6 in ~15 min and ~$0.20. Then patch the orchestrator with verified fixes (3,4,5) and relaunch. Want me to do that — provision-and-manual-debug pass — or just shotgun all the fixes and relaunch on hope?

No SSH-mediated stress on the pod, completely Ostris-supported path. Then I just scp samples and run video_understand after. 3. **Try one more orchestrator run on A100-80GB** with longer timeouts + retry logic, on the theory that earlier failures had specific bugs (which we did just fix). Might burn another $1-2 but if it works it's the most automated path. My honest read: **#2** is the fastest way to actually get a trained LoRA today. Added a "Tacit knowledge" section to `STATUS.md` with 6 groupings of stuff I learned the hard way over these runs — about ~25 individual gotchas: - **Shell + .env** — zsh mangles multi-line quoted values; bash works; `unset RUNPOD_STORAGE_NAME` is required for region-flexibility - **RunPod** — GPU id quirks (`Ada Generation` matters, `-SXM4-` matters), GraphQL 400 isn't fatal, fallback list silently escalates cost, pod death is delayed - **Ostris image** — wrong Docker repo name on Hub vs GitHub, PID 1 problem, `/etc/rp_environment` only catches `RUNPOD_*` vars, `--log` swallows stdout - **LTX 2.3** — mono-checkpoint format (not diffusers), gemma encoder hardcoded, 22B/46GB requires quantize on <80GB cards - **Orchestrator pitfalls** — cwd-upload default, wrapper returncode lies, `ship_and_run_detached(config)` always re-provisions, missing teardown on rc≠0 - **Process** — Codex sub-agent stash gotcha, megaplan `add-note` vs `feedback`, brief-snapshot semantics - **Cost discipline** — verify teardown, sample-gen failures still bill, A100 cost-per-minute math The intent is "what would I want to know if I'd never touched this stack".
